Indian Polity

From 1979-2025

UPSC PYQs - 1979
Q1. Which are the two States next to U.P. having
maximum representation in Lok Sabha?
(a) Bihar and Maharashtra
(b) Bihar and Madhya Pradesh
(c) Madhya Pradesh and Tamil Nadu
(d) Madhya Pradesh and Maharashtra
Answer: (a)
Explanation:
• The population of a state is the basis for
allocation of seats in the Lok Sabha as per the
census figures.
• The total number of seats for each state was
frozen at the 1971 census level by the 42nd
Amendment (1976) and extended till 2026 by
the 84th Amendment (2001).
• Uttar Pradesh has the maximum number of
seats (80).
• After U.P., the states with the next highest
representation are Bihar (40) and
Maharashtra (48).
• Madhya Pradesh and Tamil Nadu have fewer
seats compared to Bihar and Maharashtra.
Hence, option (a) is correct
Q2. The members of Constituent Assembly were
(a) Elected by the provincial assemblies
(b) Elected directly by the people
(c) Nominated by the government
(d) Only representatives of princely states
Answer: (a)
Explanation:
• The idea of a Constituent Assembly was first
proposed by M.N. Roy in 1934 and later
adopted by the Congress in 1935.
• It was accepted by the British Government in
the August Offer of 1940 and finally
constituted under the Cabinet Mission Plan of
1946.
• Members were indirectly elected by the
provincial legislative assemblies through the
single transferable vote system of
proportional representation.

•

Representatives of princely states were
nominated, but the main body was formed
through indirect elections.
Hence, option (a) is correct

Q3. The Vice-President of India is elected by
(a) The people directly
(b) The members of Lok Sabha and Rajya Sabha
(c) The members of Rajya Sabha only
(d) The members of Lok Sabha, Rajya Sabha and State
Legislatures
Answer: (b)
Explanation:
• The Vice-President is elected by the members
of an electoral college consisting of both
Houses of Parliament.
• The election is held by the system of
proportional representation through the
single transferable vote, and voting is by
secret ballot.
• State legislatures have no role in this
election, unlike the Presidential election.
• According to Article 66, the Vice-President
must be a citizen of India, at least 35 years
old, and qualified for election as a member of
the Rajya Sabha.
Hence, option (b) is correct
Q4. Which language has been added recently to the
Eighth Schedule of the Constitution of India?
(a) Urdu
(b) Sanskrit
(c) Sindhi
(d) Assamese
Answer: (c)
Explanation:

•

The 21st Amendment Act, 1967 added Sindhi
to the Eighth Schedule of the Constitution.
• Originally, the Eighth Schedule contained 14
languages.
• With the inclusion of Sindhi, the total became
15 languages.
• Later amendments increased the number to
22 languages through the 71st and 92nd
Amendments.
Hence, option (c) is correct

Q5. Which of the following is not included in the list
of Fundamental Duties in the Constitution?
(a) To safeguard public property and to abjure
violence
(b) To uphold and protect the sovereignty, unity and
integrity of India
(c) Secularism
(d) To abide by the Constitution and respect its ideals
Answer: (c)
Explanation:
• Fundamental Duties are listed in Article 51A,
Part IVA, inserted by the 42nd Amendment
Act, 1976.
• They include duties like respecting the
Constitution, upholding sovereignty,
protecting public property, promoting
harmony, and preserving the environment.
• “Secularism” is not a Fundamental Duty; it is
part of the Preamble and reflected in various

provisions of Fundamental Rights but not
listed as a duty.
• An additional duty regarding education for
children (6–14 years) was added by the 86th
Amendment Act, 2002.
Hence, option (c) is correct
Q6. The President of Union of India has the same
constitutional authority as the
(a) British Monarch
(b) President of U.S.A
(c) President of Egypt
(d) President of U.S.S.R
Answer: (a)
Explanation:
• India follows the parliamentary system,
where the President is a nominal executive
and the real powers are exercised by the
Council of Ministers headed by the Prime
Minister.
• This position is similar to the British Monarch,
who is the ceremonial head of state but does
not rule.
• The President acts on the aid and advice of
the Council of Ministers, and cannot act
independently in most matters.
• Unlike the U.S. President, the Indian President
does not enjoy executive powers personally.
Hence, option (a) is correct
Q7. A candidate to become a member of Lok Sabha
should not be less than
(a) 21 years
(b) 25 years
(c) 30 years
(d) 35 years
Answer: (b)
Explanation:
• As per Article 84(b) of the Constitution, a
person shall not be qualified to be chosen to
fill a seat in the Lok Sabha unless he is not
less than 25 years of age.
• For Rajya Sabha, the minimum age
requirement is 30 years.

•

•

The candidate must also be a citizen of India
and possess other qualifications prescribed by
Parliament.
Hence, option (b) is correct

The state keeps itself separate from religious
institutions, ensuring equal treatment to all
religions.
Hence, option (a) is correct

Q8. Which of the following is not included in
Fundamental Rights in the Constitution of India?
(a) Right to property
(b) Right to freedom of religion
(c) Right to vote in all elections
(d) Right to freedom of speech and expression
Answer: (a)
Explanation:
• Right to Property was originally a
Fundamental Right under Articles 19(1)(f) and
31.
• It was removed from the list of Fundamental
Rights by the 44th Amendment Act, 1978,
and is now a legal right under Article 300A in
Part XII of the Constitution.
• Right to vote is not a Fundamental Right but a
constitutional right under the Representation
of the People Act.
• Right to freedom of religion and freedom of
speech and expression continue as
Fundamental Rights under Articles 25–28 and
Article 19(1)(a) respectively.
Hence, option (a) is correct

Q10. Who is authorised to decide over a dispute
regarding disqualification of a member of
Parliament?
(a) Election Commissioner
(b) Speaker of the Lok Sabha
(c) President of India
(d) A committee set up by the Parliament
Answer: (c)
Explanation:
• Any question regarding disqualification of a
member of Parliament (other than under the
Tenth Schedule) is decided by the President.
• Before making a decision, the President is
bound to obtain the opinion of the Election
Commission, and act according to it.
• In cases of anti-defection under the Tenth
Schedule, the authority is the Speaker in the
Lok Sabha and the Chairman in the Rajya
Sabha.
• The decision of the Speaker/Chairman under
the Tenth Schedule is subject to judicial
review.
Hence, option (c) is correct

Q9. The word ‘secular’ denotes
(a) Keeping away from all religions
(b) Freedom of religion and worship to all citizens
(c) Belief in God
(d) Practising different religions
Answer: (a)
Explanation:
• A secular state has no official religion and
maintains neutrality in matters of religion.
• It does not compel citizens to adopt any
particular religion and does not give
preferential treatment to any religious group.
• In India, the word “secular” was added to the
Preamble by the 42nd Amendment Act,
1976, but secular principles existed in
Fundamental Rights from the beginning.

Q11. Which of the following States in India has no
Legislative Council?
(a) Tamil Nadu
(b) Bihar
(c) West Bengal
(d) Maharashtra
Answer: (a & c)
Explanation:
• Legislative Council (Vidhan Parishad) is the
Upper House in states with a bicameral
legislature.
• Currently, seven states have Legislative
Councils: Andhra Pradesh, Bihar, Karnataka,
Maharashtra, Telangana, Uttar Pradesh, and
West Bengal (previously had but was
abolished).

•

Tamil Nadu and West Bengal do not have a
Legislative Council at present.
• Article 169 allows Parliament to create or
abolish a Legislative Council if the State
Legislative Assembly passes a resolution by a
special majority.
Hence, options (a) and (c) are correct
Q12. The most controversial provision in the 42nd
Constitution Amendment is
(a) Supremacy of Parliament
(b) Enumeration of ten Fundamental Duties
(c) Term of Lok Sabha and Legislative Assemblies
(d) Primacy to the Directive Principles over the
Fundamental Rights
Answer: (d)
Explanation:
• The 42nd Amendment Act, 1976 gave
primacy to Directive Principles of State Policy
over Fundamental Rights by amending Article
31C.
• It extended the protection of Article 31C to all
Directive Principles, not just those in Article
39(b) and (c).
• This meant that any law made to implement
DPSPs could not be challenged even if it
violated Articles 14, 19 or 31.
• This amendment was seen as an attack on the
basic structure and judicial review, making it
highly controversial.
• The Supreme Court in Minerva Mills case
(1980) struck down this extended primacy as
unconstitutional, restoring the balance
between FRs and DPSPs.
Hence, option (d) is correct
Q13. The case of dispute in the Presidential election
is referred to
(a) Chief Election Commissioner
(b) Supreme Court
(c) Parliament
(d) None of these
Answer: (b)
Explanation:

•

Article 71 provides that all doubts and
disputes related to the election of the
President are decided by the Supreme Court.
• The Supreme Court’s decision is final and
binding.
• The election cannot be challenged on the
ground of an incomplete Electoral College.
• If the election is declared void, acts done by
the President before the declaration remain
valid.
Hence, option (b) is correct

Q14. Panchayati Raj as introduced in 1959 operates
at
(a) Samiti and block levels
(b) Block and district levels
(c) Samiti and district levels
(d) Village, block and district levels
Answer: (d)
Explanation:
• The Balwant Rai Mehta Committee (1957)
recommended a three-tier Panchayati Raj
system to ensure effective local selfgovernment.
• Based on its recommendations, Rajasthan
became the first state to launch the system on
2nd October 1959, followed by Andhra
Pradesh.
• The three levels are:
o Village level (Gram Panchayat)
o Block level (Panchayat Samiti)
o District level (Zila Parishad)
• This structure aimed at decentralisation of
power and people’s participation in rural
development.
Hence, option (d) is correct

Q15. Liberty, Equality and Fraternity, this inspiration
was derived from
(a) American Revolution
(b) French Revolution
(c) Russian Revolution
(d) None of the above
Answer: (b)
Explanation:
• The ideals of Liberty, Equality and Fraternity
were inspired by the French Revolution
(1789–1799).
• Liberty signifies freedom of thought,
expression, belief, faith and worship.
• Equality denotes the absence of special
privileges and provision of equal
opportunities.
• Fraternity stands for a sense of brotherhood
ensuring the dignity of the individual and the
unity and integrity of the nation.
• These ideals are reflected in the Preamble of
the Indian Constitution.
Hence, option (b) is correct
Q16. In 1980 Parliamentary elections which parties
got the maximum number of seats in the descending
order?
(a) Congress (I), Lok Dal, C. P. I. (M)
(b) Lok Dal, C. P. I. (M), Congress (I)
(c) D. M. K., Congress (I), Lok Dal
(d) Janata, Congress (I), C. P. I. (M)
Answer: (a)
Explanation:
• The 7th General Elections were held in
January 1980.
• Due to internal conflicts and the split within
the Janata Party, people turned back to Indira
Gandhi’s Congress (I).
• Congress (I) emerged as the largest party with
353 seats, followed by Lok Dal and CPI(M).
• Janata Party suffered a massive defeat,
winning only 31 seats.
Hence, option (a) is correct

UPSC PYQs - 1980
Q1. In which case a joint session of the Lok Sabha
and Rajya Sabha is convened by the President?
(a) When a Finance Bill is to be passed by the Lok
Sabha and the Rajya Sabha with 2/3rd majority
(b) To impeach the President
(c) If after a bill has been passed by one House and
transmitted to the other House and the bill is rejected
by the other House
(d) All of the above
Answer: (c)
Explanation:
• A joint sitting of Parliament is convened
under Article 108 to resolve a deadlock
between the two Houses over an ordinary bill.
• A deadlock arises when:
o The bill is rejected by the other
House.
o The Houses disagree on
amendments.
o More than six months elapse without
the bill being passed by the other
House.
• Money Bills and Constitutional Amendment
Bills are not subject to joint sitting.
• The Speaker of the Lok Sabha presides over
the joint sitting.
Hence, option (c) is correct
Q2. 44th Amendment of the Constitution speaks of
(a) Right to property as no longer a Fundamental Right
(b) Suspension of individual liberty during emergency
(c) Barring the courts from interfering in the disputes
regarding the election of Prime Minister
(d) Giving more importance to Directive Principles
over Fundamental Rights
Answer: (a)
Explanation:
• The 44th Amendment Act, 1978 was enacted
to reverse many provisions of the 42nd
Amendment made during the Emergency.
• It removed the Right to Property from the list
of Fundamental Rights and made it a legal
right under Article 300A.

•

It reduced the term of the Lok Sabha and
State Legislative Assemblies from six to five
years.
• It also introduced safeguards for declaring
Emergency, requiring written advice from the
Cabinet and Parliamentary approval by a twothirds majority.
Hence, option (a) is correct

Q3. The Constitution 45th Amendment Bill, passed
by Parliament extended reservation of seats for
Scheduled Castes and Scheduled Tribes in Parliament
and State Assemblies upto
(a) 1980
(b) 1985
(c) 1990
(d) 1995
Answer: (c)
Explanation:
• The 45th Amendment Act, 1980 extended the
reservation of seats for SCs and STs in
Parliament and State Legislative Assemblies
for another 10 years, i.e., up to 1990.

•

Originally, this reservation was to last for 10
years from 1950 but has been extended every
10 years through successive amendments.
• The 95th Amendment Act, 2009 further
extended this provision up to 2020.
• The reservation applies to both Lok Sabha and
State Legislative Assemblies, but elections
are held by universal adult franchise, not
separate electorates.
Hence, option (c) is correct
Q4. Council of Ministers of the Union of India is
responsible to the
(a) Parliament
(b) President
(c) Prime Minister
(d) Chief Justice
Answer: (a)
Explanation:
• Article 75(3) of the Constitution states that
the Council of Ministers shall be collectively
responsible to the Lok Sabha.
• This means all ministers stand or fall
together; if a no-confidence motion is passed
in the Lok Sabha, the entire council must
resign.
• The principle ensures parliamentary
accountability and is a key feature of the
parliamentary system of government.
• Though ministers are appointed by the
President, they remain in office as long as
they enjoy the confidence of the Lok Sabha.
Hence, option (a) is correct
Q5. What is the maximum period during which
Parliament may not meet?
(a) Six months
(b) One year
(c) Two years
(d) Three years
Answer: (a)
Explanation:
• As per Article 85, the maximum gap between
two sessions of Parliament cannot exceed six
months.

•

This ensures that Parliament meets at least
twice every year.
• Typically, Parliament has three sessions
annually:
o Budget Session (Feb–May)
o Monsoon Session (Jul–Sep)
o Winter Session (Nov–Dec)
• This provision maintains regular legislative
oversight and prevents long gaps without
parliamentary scrutiny.
Hence, option (a) is correct
Q6. Parliament consists of
(a) Prime Minister and other Ministers
(b) President, Prime Minister and other Ministers
(c) President, Rajya Sabha and Lok Sabha
(d) President, Chief Justice and Lok Sabha
Answer: (c)
Explanation:
• Article 79 of the Constitution states that the
Parliament consists of the President and the
two Houses — the Council of States (Rajya
Sabha) and the House of the People (Lok
Sabha).
• Though the President is not a member of
either House, no bill can become a law
without the President’s assent, making the
President an integral part of Parliament.
• The Prime Minister and Ministers are part of
the executive, not Parliament itself.
Hence, option (c) is correct

Q7. Rajya Sabha can delay the Money Bill passed by
the Lok Sabha for a period not exceeding
(a) 9 days
(b) 14 days
(c) 15 days
(d) 30 days
Answer: (b)

Explanation:
• As per Article 109, a Money Bill can be
introduced only in the Lok Sabha.
• After the Lok Sabha passes it, the Rajya Sabha
can only make recommendations, which the
Lok Sabha may accept or reject.
• The Rajya Sabha must return the Money Bill
within 14 days from the date of its receipt.
• If not returned within this period, the bill is
deemed to have been passed by both Houses
in the form passed by the Lok Sabha.
• Rajya Sabha cannot amend or reject a Money
Bill.
Hence, option (b) is correct
Q8. The term ‘Fourth Estate’ refers to
(a) A very backward State
(b) Judiciary
(c) Parliament
(d) Press
Answer: (d)
Explanation:
• The term “Fourth Estate” traditionally refers
to the Press or news media, especially print
journalism.
• In a democracy, media acts as the fourth
pillar, alongside the legislature, executive,
and judiciary.
• A free and independent press is essential for
ensuring transparency, accountability, and
public awareness.
• Though the Constitution does not explicitly
mention the press, its freedom is derived
from Article 19(1)(a) — freedom of speech
and expression.
Hence, option (d) is correct
Q9. Who was the second President of the Indian
Republic?
(a) Dr. Zakir Hussain
(b) Dr. S. Radhakrishnan
(c) Dr. Rajendra Prasad
(d) V. V. Giri
Answer: (b)
Explanation:

•

Dr. Sarvepalli Radhakrishnan was the second
President of India (1962–1967).
• He served as the first Vice-President from
1952 to 1962 before becoming President.
• He was a renowned philosopher, teacher,
and statesman, and was awarded the Bharat
Ratna in 1954.
• His birthday, 5th September, is celebrated as
Teachers’ Day in India.
• The first President of India was Dr. Rajendra
Prasad.
Hence, option (b) is correct
UPSC PYQs - 1981
Q1. Which of the following High Courts covers more
than one State/Union Territories?
(a) Delhi
(b) Allahabad
(c) Guwahati
(d) None of these
Answer: (c)
Explanation:
• The Guwahati High Court (formerly the
Assam High Court) has jurisdiction over
multiple North-Eastern states.
• It was established in 1948 and initially served
as the High Court for the entire North-East.
• Currently, it exercises jurisdiction over Assam,
Nagaland, Mizoram, and Arunachal Pradesh.
• Separate High Courts have been created later
for some UTs and states, but Guwahati
remains the only High Court with multi-state
jurisdiction.
Hence, option (c) is correct

Q2. The Constitution of India was actually ready in
(a) 1947
(b) 1949
(c) 1950
(d) 1952
Answer: (b)
Explanation:
• The Constituent Assembly took 2 years, 11
months, and 17 days to draft the
Constitution.
• It was adopted on 26 November 1949, after
thorough debates and discussions spread
across 11 sessions.
• The Constitution came into force on 26
January 1950, to commemorate the Purna
Swaraj Declaration of 1930.
• 26 November is now celebrated as
Constitution Day in India.
Hence, option (b) is correct
Q3. Panchayati Raj System was introduced in the
year
(a) 1948
(b) 1959
(c) 1951
(d) 1952
Answer: (b)
Explanation:
• The Balwant Rai Mehta Committee (1957)
recommended the establishment of a threetier Panchayati Raj system — at the village,
block, and district levels.
• On 2nd October 1959, Rajasthan became the
first state to introduce the system, followed
by Andhra Pradesh nine days later.
• The aim was to promote decentralisation of
power and ensure people’s participation in
rural development.
• The system was later constitutionalised
through the 73rd Amendment Act, 1992.
Hence, option (b) is correct

Q4. The dispute regarding the election of Prime
Minister and President of India can be decided by
(a) High Court
(b) Election Tribunal
(c) Chief Election Commissioner
(d) None of the above
Answer: (d)
Explanation:
• Disputes regarding the election of the
President are decided exclusively by the
Supreme Court under Article 71, and its
decision is final.
• For the Prime Minister, disputes are governed
by the Representation of the People Act,
1951, and are dealt with by the High Courts
through election petitions, not by a separate
tribunal.
• There is no Election Tribunal for these
matters now.
• Therefore, none of the given options directly
matches both scenarios together.
Hence, option (d) is correct
Q5. Rajya Sabha is dissolved once in
(a) 2 years
(b) 3 years
(c) 6 years
(d) It is never dissolved
Answer: (d)
Explanation:
• The Rajya Sabha is a permanent House and is
not subject to dissolution.
• It was first constituted in 1952 and continues
to function uninterrupted.
• One-third of its members retire every two
years, and new members are elected to fill
those vacancies.
• A member elected for a full term serves for six
years.
• This system ensures continuity in the
legislative process, unlike the Lok Sabha
which can be dissolved.
Hence, option (d) is correct

Q6. Directive Principles of State Policy are contained
in
(a) Part I of the Constitution
(b) Part II of the Constitution
(c) Part III of the Constitution
(d) Part IV of the Constitution
Answer: (d)
Explanation:
• Directive Principles of State Policy (DPSPs)
are enumerated in Part IV (Articles 36 to 51)
of the Constitution.
• They are non-justiciable, meaning they are
not enforceable in a court of law, but they are
fundamental in the governance of the
country.
• Inspired by the Irish Constitution, DPSPs aim
to create a welfare state by guiding the state
in policy-making.
• They include socialistic, Gandhian, and
liberal-intellectual principles, such as
promoting equal justice, organising village
panchayats, and securing a uniform civil code.
Hence, option (d) is correct

Q7. China has which type of Parliamentary System?
(a) One party
(b) Two-party
(c) Multi-party
(d) None of these
Answer: (c)
Explanation:
• China follows a multi-party cooperation and
political consultation system under the
leadership of the Communist Party of China
(CPC).

•

While the CPC is the only ruling party, there
are eight other smaller political parties that
participate in governance through the
Chinese People’s Political Consultative
Conference (CPPCC).
• These parties function under the precondition
of accepting CPC’s leadership, making it a
multi-party system with one dominant party.
• The National People’s Congress is the highest
organ of state power in China.
Hence, option (c) is correct
Q8. What does secularism mean?
(a) State patronising all religions
(b) State having its own religion
(c) State having no official religion and citizens are
free to follow any religion or faith
(d) State having one official religion and showing
respect to all other religions
Answer: (c)
Explanation:
• In India, secularism means that the State has
no official religion and treats all religions with
equal respect.
• Citizens have the freedom to profess, practise
and propagate any religion of their choice as
guaranteed by Article 25.
• The word “secular” was explicitly added to
the Preamble by the 42nd Amendment Act,
1976, though secular principles existed since
1950 through Fundamental Rights.
• The Supreme Court in S.R. Bommai v. Union
of India (1994) declared secularism as part of
the basic structure of the Constitution.
Hence, option (c) is correct
Q9. Which of the following is not a Fundamental
Right?
(a) Right to equality
(b) Right to property
(c) Right against exploitation
(d) Right to freedom of speech and expression
Answer: (b)
Explanation:

•

Right to Property was originally a
Fundamental Right under Articles 19(1)(f) and
31, but it was removed by the 44th
Amendment Act, 1978.
• It is now a legal right under Article 300A, Part
XII of the Constitution.
• Right to equality (Articles 14–18), right
against exploitation (Articles 23–24), and
freedom of speech and expression (Article
19(1)(a)) continue to be Fundamental Rights.
• The removal of right to property helped the
State implement land reforms and social
welfare legislation without legal hurdles.
Hence, option (b) is correct
Q10. On which date Constitution of India was
adopted and enacted by the Constituent Assembly?
(a) 15th August, 1947
(b) 26th January, 1950
(c) 26th November, 1949
(d) 30th January, 1948
Answer: (c)
Explanation:
• The Constituent Assembly adopted and
enacted the Constitution of India on 26th
November 1949.
• This date is mentioned in the Preamble as the
day when “We, the people of India, adopted,
enacted and gave to ourselves this
Constitution.”
• The Constitution came into effect on 26th
January 1950, chosen to commemorate the
Purna Swaraj declaration of 1930.
• 26 November is celebrated as Constitution
Day (Samvidhan Divas) in India since 2015.
Hence, option (c) is correct

530 members representing the states,
20 representing Union Territories,
o and 2 nominated Anglo-Indians
(when applicable).
• This ensured inclusive representation in the
lower house.
Hence, option (a) is correct

Q1. President of India is elected by
(a) Lok Sabha
(b) Rajya Sabha
(c) Elected representatives of Lok Sabha, Rajya Sabha
and State Legislative Assemblies
(d) People directly
Answer: (c)
Explanation:
• The President is elected by an Electoral
College consisting of:
o Elected members of both Houses of
Parliament, and
o Elected members of the Legislative
Assemblies of the States, including
NCT of Delhi and Puducherry.
• The election is held by proportional
representation through the single
transferable vote system.
• The value of votes is calculated based on the
population of states as per the 1971 Census
(frozen till 2026).
• Nominated members of Parliament and State
Assemblies do not participate in the election.
Hence, option (c) is correct
Q2. How many members can be nominated to the
Lok Sabha by the President?
(a) 2
(b) 4
(c) 5
(d) 12
Answer: (a)
Explanation:
• As per Article 331, the President could
nominate up to 2 members of the AngloIndian community to the Lok Sabha if he/she
felt the community was not adequately
represented.
• This provision was abolished by the 104th
Constitutional Amendment Act, 2019, which
ended the nomination from January 2020.
• The maximum strength of the Lok Sabha is
552:

Q3. If President wants to resign, he should address
his resignation letter to the
(a) Parliament
(b) Prime Minister
(c) Chief Justice of India
(d) Vice-President
Answer: (d)
Explanation:
• As per Article 56(1)(a), the President may
resign from office by writing under his hand
to the Vice-President.
• The President holds office for five years, but
can resign earlier or be removed through
impeachment.
• The resignation letter takes effect from the
date it is accepted.
• If the office of the President falls vacant, the
Vice-President acts as President until a new
President is elected.
Hence, option (d) is correct
Q4. When a money bill is passed by the Parliament,
the President has the power to
(a) Amend it
(b) Reject it
(c) Kept it with him
(d) Return it for reconsideration
Answer: (c)
Explanation:
• Under Article 110, a Money Bill can only be
introduced in the Lok Sabha and requires the
President’s prior recommendation.
• Once passed by Parliament, the President
cannot return a Money Bill for
reconsideration.

•

The President must give assent, but he may
withhold assent (i.e., keep it pending) —
known as absolute veto.
• This is different from ordinary bills, where the
President has returning powers (suspensive
veto).
Hence, option (c) is correct
Q5. Directive Principles of State Policy are included
in which part of the Indian Constitution?
(a) Part IV
(b) Part III
(c) Part II
(d) Part I
Answer: (a)
Explanation:
• Directive Principles of State Policy (DPSPs)
are contained in Part IV of the Constitution,
from Articles 36 to 51.
• They are borrowed from the Irish
Constitution and aim to establish social and
economic democracy.
• DPSPs are non-justiciable, but they are
fundamental in governance, providing
guidance for making laws and policies to build
a welfare state.
• They include socialistic, Gandhian and liberalintellectual principles.
Hence, option (a) is correct
Q6. Finance Commission is appointed for
(a) Passing the money bills
(b) Approving money bills
(c) Drafting the budget
(d) Making recommendations to the President
regarding the distribution between the Union and the
States of the net proceeds of the taxes
Answer: (d)
Explanation:
• The Finance Commission is a constitutional
body set up under Article 280 every five years
by the President.
• Its main function is to recommend the
distribution of tax revenues between the

Union and the States and among the States
themselves.
• It also recommends principles governing
grants-in-aid and measures to augment state
finances.
• It acts as the balancing wheel of fiscal
federalism in India.
Hence, option (d) is correct
Q7. Panchayati Raj is mainly aimed at
(a) Rural employment
(b) Agricultural and rural industries development
(c) Political awareness among village people
(d) Giving training to villagers for fighting elections
Answer: (c)
Explanation:
• Panchayati Raj signifies the system of rural
local self-government in India.
• Its main aim is to decentralise power and
promote political awareness and
participation at the grassroots level.
• It ensures people’s participation in decisionmaking for rural development.
• The system was introduced after the Balwant
Rai Mehta Committee (1957)
recommendations and formally launched in
1959.
Hence, option (c) is correct

Q8. Which one of the following is not a Fundamental
Right?
(a) Freedom of speech and expression
(b) Right to assemble peaceably and without arms
(c) Right to property
(d) Right to equality
Answer: (c)
Explanation:

•

Originally, the Right to Property was a
Fundamental Right under Articles 19(1)(f)
and 31.
• The 44th Constitutional Amendment Act,
1978 removed it from the list of Fundamental
Rights.
• It is now a legal/constitutional right under
Article 300A in Part XII.
• Other rights such as freedom of speech (Art.
19), right to equality (Art. 14), and right to
assemble (Art. 19) continue to be
Fundamental Rights.
Hence, option (c) is correct
UPSC PYQs - 1983
Q1. A certain bill was passed by a State legislature in
August 1982 but did not receive the assent of the
State Governor. It was
(a) Bihar Press Bill
(b) Jammu & Kashmir Resettlement Bill
(c) Tamil Nadu Press Bill
(d) Orissa Land Reforms Bill
Answer: (a)
Explanation:
• The Bihar Press Bill, 1982 was passed by the
Bihar State Legislature but the Governor did
not give his assent.
• The bill sought to curb indecent or scurrilous
publications, making it a criminal offence to
publish or possess such material.
• It was seen as a serious threat to the freedom
of the press, drawing widespread criticism.
• Under Article 200, a Governor may give
assent, withhold assent, or reserve the bill
for the President’s consideration.
Hence, option (a) is correct
Q2. The regional political party which has the largest
number of members in the Lok Sabha is
(a) Telugu Desam
(b) DMK
(c) Akali Dal
(d) AIADMK
Answer: (a)

Explanation:
• Telugu Desam Party (TDP), a regional party
from Andhra Pradesh, emerged as a strong
force in the early 1980s under the leadership
of N.T. Rama Rao.
• In the 1984 Lok Sabha elections, TDP won 30
seats, becoming the largest opposition party
in the Lok Sabha at that time.
• This marked a significant rise of regional
parties in national politics.
Hence, option (a) is correct
Q3. The Nagaland State of India cannot be termed a
State because it lacks
(a) Linguistic homogeneity
(b) Sovereignty
(c) Bicameral legislature
(d) A High Court exclusively ‘of its own’
Answer: (b)
Explanation:
• Nagaland became the 16th state of India in
1963, but like all Indian states, it does not
possess sovereignty, which lies with the
Union of India.
• It has its own legislature and administrative
structure but remains subject to the
Constitution of India.
• Sovereignty is the defining attribute of a state
in the international sense, which Indian
states, including Nagaland, do not have.
• Options like linguistic homogeneity or
bicameral legislature are not essential criteria
for statehood under the Indian Constitution.
Hence, option (b) is correct
Q4. The reorganisation of Indian States on a linguistic
basis took place in
(a) 1947
(b) 1950
(c) 1956
(d) 1962
Answer: (c)
Explanation:
• The States Reorganisation Act, 1956 came
into effect on 1 November 1956, reorganising

the boundaries of India's states and territories
primarily on linguistic lines.
• It followed the recommendations of the Fazl
Ali Commission (1953), which examined
demands for linguistic states after the
creation of Andhra State (1953) for Teluguspeaking people.
• The Act divided the country into 14 states and
6 union territories.
• This marked a major shift in the federal
structure of India.
Hence, option (c) is correct
Q5. The greatest hallmark of personal liberty is
(a) Mandamus
(b) Certiorari
(c) Quo Warranto
(d) Habeas Corpus
Answer: (d)
Explanation:
• The writ of Habeas Corpus literally means “to
have the body”.
• It is issued by the courts to produce a person
who has been detained unlawfully, ensuring
protection of personal liberty.
• It can be filed by any person on behalf of the
detainee, making it a powerful tool against
arbitrary detention.
• Other writs like Mandamus, Certiorari, and
Quo Warranto serve different administrative
or judicial control functions.
• Habeas Corpus is considered the bulwark of
individual freedom in a democracy.
Hence, option (d) is correct
Q6. Which one of the following is not a piece of
social legislation?
(a) The Immoral Offences and Girls Trafficking Act
(b) The Maintenance of Internal Security Act
(c) The Abolition of Untouchability (Offences) Act
(d) The Special Marriage Act
Answer: (b)
Explanation:
• The Maintenance of Internal Security Act
(MISA), 1971 was a law giving the

government extensive powers for preventive
detention, search, and wiretapping, mainly
used during the Emergency (1975–77).
• It was aimed at internal security and political
control, not at bringing social reform.
• The other Acts are social legislations:
o Immoral Traffic (Prevention) Act,
1956 – combats trafficking and
exploitation.
o Abolition of Untouchability
(Offences) Act, 1955 – enforces
Article 17.
o Special Marriage Act, 1954 – provides
legal framework for inter-religious
and civil marriages.
Hence, option (b) is correct

Q7. The Jammu and Kashmir Resettlement Bill which
has stirred up controversy deals with
(a) Reorganisation of territories between Jammu &
Kashmir
(b) Allowing people of Kashmiri origin who have
migrated to Pakistan to come back to Jammu &
Kashmir
(c) Introduction of new land reforms in the State
(d) Imposition of agricultural income tax in the State
Answer: (b)
Explanation:

•

The Jammu and Kashmir Resettlement Act,
1982 provided for the resettlement of
persons of Kashmiri origin who migrated to
Pakistan after March 1947, allowing them to
return and reclaim property in J&K.
• The Act became controversial due to security
and citizenship concerns, as it could allow
large numbers of Pakistanis (including
potentially hostile elements) to enter the
state.
• The Supreme Court stayed the
implementation of the Act, and the matter
remained unresolved for years.
• The Governor initially returned the Bill, but it
was passed again without changes, leading to
a Presidential Reference to the Supreme
Court.
Hence, option (b) is correct
Q8. It is often said that the key to the minds of the
makers of our Constitution lies in
(a) Preamble
(b) Fundamental Rights
(c) Fundamental Duties
(d) Directive Principles of State Policy
Answer: (a)
Explanation:
• The Preamble embodies the philosophy,
values, and objectives that guided the
framers of the Constitution.
• It is based on the Objectives Resolution
moved by Jawaharlal Nehru in 1946.
• The Supreme Court in the Berubari case
(1960) observed that the Preamble is a key to
open the mind of the makers of the
Constitution.
• It sets out the ideals of justice, liberty,
equality, fraternity, and defines the nature of
the Indian State as sovereign, socialist,
secular, and democratic republic.
• It also serves as an interpretative tool when
there is ambiguity in constitutional provisions.
Hence, option (a) is correct

UPSC PYQs - 1984
Q1. Sarkaria Commission has been set up to study
(a) Centre-State relations
(b) Inter-State relations
(c) Financial aid to States
(d) Splitting of LIC
Answer: (a)
Explanation:
• The Sarkaria Commission was constituted in
1983 under the chairmanship of Justice R.S.
Sarkaria.
• It was set up to review the Centre–State
relations and suggest reforms to strengthen
cooperative federalism.
• Key recommendations included:
o Establishing a permanent Inter-State
Council under Article 263.
o Using Article 356 (President’s Rule)
only as a last resort.
o Promoting consultation with States
before legislating on Concurrent List
subjects.
o Reactivating Zonal Councils for better
coordination.
Hence, option (a) is correct
Q2. The Eighth Schedule of Indian Constitution
contains
(a) Fundamental Rights
(b) Directive Principles
(c) A list of 18 languages recognised by the
Constitution
(d) Number of States and Union Territories
Answer: (c)
Explanation:
• The Eighth Schedule of the Constitution lists
the languages recognised by the
Constitution.
• It originally contained 14 languages, and over
time, more were added through
amendments:
o Sindhi was added in 1967 (21st
Amendment).

o

Konkani, Manipuri, Nepali were
added in 1992 (71st Amendment).
o Bodo, Santhali, Maithili, Dogri were
added in 2004 (92nd Amendment).
• As of now, the total number of languages is
22.
• The Government is responsible for the
development of these languages under
Article 351.
Hence, option (c) is correct
Q3. Match the following:
A. Chief Election Commissioner
B. Speaker of Lok Sabha
C. Deputy Chairman of Rajya Sabha
1. Elected by members of Rajya Sabha
2. Appointed by the President
3. Elected by members of Lok Sabha
Select the correct answer from the codes given below:
(a) A—1, B—2, C—3
(b) A—2, B—3, C—1
(c) A—3, B—2, C—1
(d) None of these
Answer: (b)
Explanation:
• Chief Election Commissioner → Appointed by
the President (Article 324).
• Speaker of Lok Sabha → Elected by members
of Lok Sabha in its first sitting by simple
majority.
• Deputy Chairman of Rajya Sabha → Elected
by members of Rajya Sabha from amongst
themselves.
• These positions ensure the independence of
constitutional bodies and the smooth
functioning of Parliament.
Hence, option (b) is correct
Q4. Mandal Commission report pertains to
(a) Backward Classes
(b) Minorities
(c) Scheduled Castes
(d) Scheduled Tribes
Answer: (a)
Explanation:

•

The Mandal Commission was set up in 1979
by the Janata Party Government under Prime
Minister Morarji Desai, chaired by
Bindheshwari Prasad Mandal.
• Its purpose was to identify socially and
educationally backward classes and
recommend measures for their advancement.
• It recommended 27% reservation in
government jobs for Other Backward Classes
(OBCs), so that total reservations for SCs, STs,
and OBCs reached 50%.
• The recommendations were implemented in
1990 by the V.P. Singh Government, which
led to major political and social changes.
Hence, option (a) is correct
Q5. A good citizen is one who is
(a) Conscious of social obligations
(b) Not bothered about anything
(c) Educated
(d) Rich in cultural heritage
Answer: (a)
Explanation:
• A good citizen is expected to be aware of and
fulfil social obligations such as respecting
laws, paying taxes, and participating actively
in democratic processes.
• Citizenship involves both rights and duties;
being socially conscious ensures harmony and
collective progress.
• Merely being educated or culturally rich does
not ensure responsible citizenship without
active contribution to society.
Hence, option (a) is correct
Q6. Centre-State financial relations are looked after
by the
(a) Finance Commission
(b) Sarkaria Commission
(c) Ministry of Finance
(d) Planning Commission
Answer: (a)
Explanation:
• The Finance Commission, established under
Article 280, is a constitutional body

responsible for recommending the
distribution of financial resources between
the Union and the States.
• It is constituted by the President every five
years or earlier if necessary.
• Its key functions include:
o Distribution of net proceeds of taxes
between the Centre and States.
o Laying down the principles for grantsin-aid to States.
o Recommending measures to augment
State finances.
• It plays a crucial role in maintaining fiscal
federalism in India.
Hence, option (a) is correct
Q7. Who selects persons for appointment to the
Central Civil Services in India?
(a) President
(b) Prime Minister
(c) Planning Commission
(d) UPSC
Answer: (d)
Explanation:
• The Union Public Service Commission (UPSC),
established under Articles 315–323, is the
central recruiting agency for appointments to
the All India Services, Central Services, and
services of Union Territories.
• It conducts various competitive examinations
such as Civil Services Examination,
Engineering Services, etc.
• While the President makes the formal
appointment, the selection is carried out by
UPSC, ensuring independence, meritocracy,
and transparency in recruitment.
• UPSC is a constitutional body, independent of
the executive.
Hence, option (d) is correct
Q8. Panchayati Raj system was adopted to
(a) Make people aware of politics
(b) Decentralise the power of democracy
(c) Educate the peasants

(d) To introduce a form of local self-government at
the village, block and district levels
Answer: (b)
Explanation:
• The Panchayati Raj system was adopted to
promote decentralisation of power and
strengthen grassroots democracy.
• It aims to transfer decision-making powers
from the Centre and States to local bodies,
enabling people’s participation in
governance.
• Based on the Balwant Rai Mehta Committee
(1957) recommendations, the system was first
implemented in 1959 in Rajasthan.
• Later, the 73rd Constitutional Amendment
Act, 1992 gave it constitutional status,
mandating a three-tier structure — village,
block, and district levels.
Hence, option (b) is correct
Q9. Democracy exists in India. Government is run by
the elected representatives of the people. For proper
functioning of this system
(a) One should cast vote in elections
(b) One must be prompt in paying taxes
(c) One must be good to himself and to the society
(d) None of these
Answer: (a)
Explanation:
• Democracy functions effectively only when
citizens actively participate in elections by
exercising their right to vote.
• Voting enables people to choose their
representatives, hold them accountable, and
shape the direction of governance.
• While paying taxes and being socially
responsible are important civic duties, voting
is the primary mechanism through which
democracy operates in a representative
system like India.
• Universal adult franchise is the foundation of
India’s democratic structure.
Hence, option (a) is correct

Q10. Opposition conclave at Srinagar forwarded the
following recommendations:
A. Government at the Centre should be shared by all
parties
B. Governor should be appointed from a panel
forwarded by the State Government concerned
C. Setting up of Inter-State Council to finalise disputes
between the states
D. Giving more powers to the States
E. Articles 356 and 357 should be suitably amended
F. Resignation of Central Government
Out of these only:
(a) A and B are true
(b) A, C and D are true
(c) C, D, E and F are true
(d) C and E are true
Answer: (a)
Explanation:
• The Opposition Conclave at Srinagar (1983–
84) was held to highlight issues of CentreState relations and the dominance of the
Centre in Indian federalism.
• Key demands included:
o Sharing power at the Centre among
all parties to prevent centralisation.
o Appointment of Governors from a
panel suggested by the respective
State Government to reduce bias.
• Other options like amendments to Articles
356/357 or creation of Inter-State Council
were discussed later in different forums like
the Sarkaria Commission.
Hence, option (a) is correct
Q11. Which is correct?
(a) Lok Sabha cannot be dissolved before 5 years
(b) Rajya Sabha lasts only for 6 years
(c) Lok Sabha can be dissolved before 5 years
(d) Life of Lok Sabha can be extended indefinitely
Answer: (c)
Explanation:
• The normal term of the Lok Sabha is 5 years
from the date of its first sitting (Article 83).

•

However, the President can dissolve the Lok
Sabha earlier on the advice of the Council of
Ministers, or if no government can be formed.
• During a national emergency, Parliament may
extend the term of Lok Sabha by one year at a
time, but not beyond 6 months after the
emergency ends.
• The Rajya Sabha, on the other hand, is a
permanent house and is not subject to
dissolution; one-third of its members retire
every 2 years.
Hence, option (c) is correct
UPSC PYQs - 1985
Q1. Consider the following States with respect to
their representation to the Lok Sabha:
I. Andhra Pradesh
II. Madhya Pradesh
III. Tamil Nadu
IV. Maharashtra
Which of the following sequences is correct? (>
means greater than)
(a) IV > I > III > II
(b) I > II > III > IV
(c) IV > III > II > I
(d) II > I > III > IV
Answer: (a)
Explanation:
• Allocation basis: Seats are allotted broadly in
proportion to population, and the numbers
were frozen at 1971 census levels (42nd &
84th Amendments) till 2026.
• Seat counts (historical/frozen): Maharashtra
48, Andhra Pradesh 42 (pre-bifurcation),
Tamil Nadu 39, Madhya Pradesh 29 (preChhattisgarh).
• Why not others: TN (39) is lower than AP (42),
and MP (29) is lowest among the four, so any
order putting MP before TN/AP is wrong.
• Correct descending order: Maharashtra (IV) >
Andhra Pradesh (I) > Tamil Nadu (III) >
Madhya Pradesh (II).
Hence, option (a) is correct

Q2. The President of India is elected indirectly by the
electoral college consisting of the elected members
of the
(a) Lok Sabha only
(b) Rajya Sabha only
(c) Lok Sabha and the Rajya Sabha only
(d) Lok Sabha, the Rajya Sabha and the Legislative
Assemblies of the States
Answer: (d)
Explanation:
• Electoral College: Elected members of both
Houses of Parliament + elected MLAs of all
States and of NCT of Delhi & Puducherry.
• Excluded: Nominated members (both at
Centre and States) do not vote in the
Presidential election.
• Voting system: Proportional Representation
by Single Transferable Vote; secret ballot.
• Vote value equalisation: Formula ensures
uniformity among States and parity between
States and the Union (Article 55).
Hence, option (d) is correct
Q3. In which of the following States in India, the
number of electorate is the highest?
(a) Bihar
(b) Uttar Pradesh
(c) Karnataka
(d) Rajasthan
Answer: (b)
Explanation:
• Electorate means the total number of
registered voters; it correlates strongly with
population.
• Uttar Pradesh is India’s most populous state,
therefore it has the largest voter list.
• Bihar, Rajasthan, Karnataka have smaller
populations/electorates than U.P., so they
trail behind.
Hence, option (b) is correct
Q4. In a Parliamentary System of Government
(a) The parliament is responsible to the executive
(b) The parliament is responsible to the judiciary

(c) The executive is responsible to the parliament
(d) The judiciary is responsible to the parliament
Answer: (c)
Explanation:
• Core doctrine: Collective responsibility of the
Council of Ministers to the Lok Sabha (Article
75(3)).
• Practical control tools: Question Hour, Zero
Hour, censure & no-confidence motions,
calling attention, adjournment motions,
committee oversight.
• Consequence: If the ministry loses majority or
a no-confidence motion passes, it must resign
or seek dissolution.
• Why others are wrong: Parliament isn’t
responsible to the executive/judiciary;
judiciary reviews legality, not policy.
Hence, option (c) is correct

Q5. After retirement, a High Court Judge
(a) Can plead in the same High Court
(b) Cannot plead in the same High Court
(c) Can plead in the Supreme Court
(d) Can plead in any of the High Courts
Answer: (b)
Explanation:
• Article 220: A permanent High Court judge,
after retirement, shall not plead or act in the
High Court in which he served (to avoid
conflict of interest and maintain impartiality).
• Permissible practice: Such a retired judge
may appear in the Supreme Court or in other
High Courts (but not the same one).
• Trap in options: While (c) and (d) mention
where they can practise, the question asks

what is barred; hence the correct choice is
the prohibition.
Hence, option (b) is correct
Q6. The Panchayati Raj was first introduced in the
States of
(a) Rajasthan and Andhra Pradesh
(b) Bihar
(c) Gujarat
(d) Haryana
Answer: (a)
Explanation:
• Balwant Rai Mehta Committee (1957)
recommended a three-tier Panchayati Raj for
grassroots democracy.
• Inauguration: 2 October 1959, Nagaur
(Rajasthan) by Jawaharlal Nehru.
• Second state: Andhra Pradesh, 9 days later at
Shadnagar.
• Constitutional status: Later entrenched via
the 73rd Constitutional Amendment (1992),
adding Part IX & Eleventh Schedule.
Hence, option (a) is correct
Q7. Which of the following languages are included in
the Eighth Schedule of the Indian Constitution?
(a) Sindhi and English
(b) English and Kashmiri
(c) Kashmiri and Konkani
(d) Sindhi and Kashmiri
Answer: (c) & (d)
Explanation:
• The Eighth Schedule originally contained 14
languages, and over the years, more were
added through Constitutional Amendments.
• Sindhi was added by the 21st Amendment
Act, 1967.
• Kashmiri, Konkani and Nepali were added by
the 71st Amendment Act, 1992.
• English is not listed in the Eighth Schedule —
it's an official language, but not a scheduled
language.
• Therefore, the correct combinations from the
given options are:
o (c) Kashmiri and Konkani

o (d) Sindhi and Kashmiri
Hence, options (c) and (d) are correct

Q8. The Seventh Schedule of the Indian Constitution
contains
(a) States and Union Territories
(b) Salaries of the President, Governors of States,
Judges of Supreme Court, etc.
(c) Union List, State List and Concurrent List
(d) Allocation of seats in the Rajya Sabha
Answer: (c)
Explanation:
• Seventh Schedule provides for three-fold
distribution of legislative subjects between
the Centre and the States:
o List I – Union List (currently 100
subjects): Parliament has exclusive
power (e.g., defence, banking, foreign
affairs).
o List II – State List (currently 61
subjects): States have exclusive
power in normal times (e.g., police,
public health, agriculture).
o List III – Concurrent List (currently 52
subjects): Both Parliament and States
can legislate; in case of conflict,
Parliament’s law prevails (e.g.,
criminal law, marriage & divorce,
education).
• This division is inspired by the Government of
India Act, 1935 and ensures a federal
legislative structure.
Hence, option (c) is correct
Q9. The National Anthem was written by
(a) Rabindranath Tagore
(b) Bankim Chandra Chatterji
(c) Mohammed Iqbal
(d) None of the above
Answer: (a)
Explanation:
• “Jana Gana Mana” was originally composed
in Bengali by Rabindranath Tagore.

•

It was first sung on 27 December 1911 at the
Calcutta session of the Indian National
Congress.
• It was adopted as the National Anthem by the
Constituent Assembly on 24 January 1950.
• The full song has five stanzas; the playing
time of the full version is approximately 52
seconds.
• Tagore himself translated the song into
English.
• (b) Bankim Chandra wrote “Vande Mataram”,
not Jana Gana Mana.
Hence, option (a) is correct

Q10. What is the minimum percentage of the
strength of opposition required in a State legislature
for the appointment of an opposition leader?
(a) 20%
(b) 25%
(c) 40%
(d) None of the above
Answer: (d)
Explanation:
• There is no specific constitutional or legal
requirement for a minimum percentage for
appointment of a Leader of Opposition.
• However, by parliamentary convention, a
party must secure at least 10% of the total
strength of the House to get official
recognition as the Opposition Party and
thereby have a Leader of the Opposition.
• If no party meets this criterion, no formal
Leader of the Opposition is appointed.
• This rule applies both at the Union and State
levels.

Hence, option (d) is correct
Q11. Who among the following is/are member(s) of
Parliament?
A. Shivaji Ganesan
B. Amitabh Bachchan
C. Sunil Dutt
D. Vyjayanthimala
E. Jayalalitha
(a) A and B only
(b) B, C, D and E only
(c) All of A, B, C, D and E
(d) A, B, C and D only
Answer: (c)
Explanation:
• Shivaji Ganesan → Nominated to Rajya Sabha
in 1982 after the death of Nargis.
• Amitabh Bachchan → Elected to Lok Sabha
from Allahabad in 1984 on a Congress ticket
with record victory margin.
• Sunil Dutt → Elected to 8th Lok Sabha from
Mumbai North-West on Congress ticket.
• Vyjayanthimala → Elected twice to Lok Sabha
and once nominated to Rajya Sabha.
• Jayalalitha → Elected as Member of
Parliament (Rajya Sabha) before becoming
CM of Tamil Nadu.
All five have served in Parliament at some point.
Hence, option (c) is correct
UPSC PYQs - 1986
Q1. The most irritating factor in the Centre–State
relationship is the
(a) River water dispute
(b) Interference of the Centre in the functions allotted
to them by the Constitution
(c) Allocation of finances
(d) None of the above
Answer: (b)
Explanation:
• River water disputes are generally interstate, not between Centre and States directly.
The Centre usually acts as arbitrator (e.g.,
Cauvery, Krishna).

•

Financial allocation is guided by the Finance
Commission (Art. 280), a constitutional body,
reducing scope for arbitrary interference.
• The real friction arises when the Centre
interferes in State matters—especially
through provisions like:
o Article 356: Imposition of President’s
Rule, often on political grounds.
o Article 249 & 250: Parliament
legislating on State List matters in
national interest or during
emergencies.
o Governor’s discretionary powers,
often seen as an agent of the Centre.
• These central interventions have historically
been major irritants in Centre–State relations.
Hence, option (b) is correct
Q2. Mandamus means
(a) A writ issued by a superior court commanding that
a specified thing be done
(b) A legal term for a prisoner’s right to appear in
person and be tried in court
(c) A written public declaration of the intentions,
opinions or motives of a sovereign or of a leader,
party or body
(d) The principle of hybridisation, discovered by
Gregor Mendel which led to the improved breeds of
plant and animal life
Answer: (a)
Explanation:
• Mandamus literally means “We command”.
• It is issued by a higher court to a public
authority, corporation, tribunal, or inferior
court, directing them to perform a
public/legal duty which they have failed or
refused to perform.
• It is called a “wakening call” — it wakes up
authorities from inaction.
• Limitations: It cannot be issued against:
o Private bodies,
o The President or Governors,
o Chief Justices acting in judicial
capacity,

o

To enforce non-statutory
departmental instructions, or
o When duty is discretionary.
• It is one of the five writs under Article 32 &
226.
Hence, option (a) is correct
Q3. The main function of the National Development
Council is
(a) To promote national integration
(b) To develop villages
(c) To review the working of the National Plan from
time to time
(d) None of the above
Answer: (c)
Explanation:
• National Development Council (NDC) was set
up in August 1952 by an executive resolution,
not by the Constitution.
• Composition: PM (Chairman), Union
Ministers, Chief Ministers, Lt. Governors of
UTs, and members of the Planning
Commission.
• Main functions:
o Prescribe guidelines for the
preparation of National Plan.
o Consider and approve the National
Plan.
o Assess resources required for the
Plan.
o Consider policy questions affecting
national development.
o Review the working of the Plan from
time to time.
o Recommend measures for achieving
Plan targets.
• It served as a bridge between the Centre and
States in planning.
Hence, option (c) is correct
Q4. Sarkaria Commission was set up to
(a) Investigate about the riots that took place in Delhi
in 1984
(b) Maintain Centre–State relationship
(c) Make necessary investigations and suggestions

regarding the problem of Punjab
(d) Investigate about the assassination of Mrs. Indira
Gandhi
Answer: (b)
Explanation:
• Sarkaria Commission was constituted in 1983
under Justice R.S. Sarkaria to review Centre–
State relations and recommend reforms.
• Key recommendations:
o Set up a permanent Inter-State
Council (Article 263) → later
implemented.
o Use Article 356 sparingly.
o Promote consultation with States
before legislating on Concurrent List.
o Strengthen Zonal Councils.
• It was not related to Punjab, riots, or Indira
Gandhi’s assassination.
Hence, option (b) is correct

Q5. Asom Gana Parishad was recently in the news
because
(a) Of the 64 elected members, 52 are bachelors
(b) All the 64 legislators are in the age group of 25 to
35
(c) All the legislators have been elected for the first
time
(d) All of the above
Answer: (d)
Explanation:
• Asom Gana Parishad (AGP) emerged from the
six-year-long Assam Movement (1979–1985)
led by AASU & AAGSP, demanding detection
and deportation of illegal migrants.
• After the Assam Accord (1985), AGP
contested the state elections in December

1985 and won a landslide, forming the
government.
• Notable facts about AGP MLAs:
o Majority were young first-time
legislators.
o Many were bachelors.
o All belonged to 25–35 age group,
marking a generational shift in
Assamese politics.
• Prafulla Kumar Mahanta, aged 33, became
India’s youngest CM at the time.
Hence, option (d) is correct
Q6. The Directive Principles of State Policy have been
given precedence over Fundamental Rights in the ....
Constitutional Amendment
(a) 41st
(b) 42nd
(c) 43rd
(d) 45th
Answer: (b)
Explanation:
• The 42nd Constitutional Amendment Act,
1976 is also called the “Mini Constitution”
because of its wide-ranging changes.
• It gave Directive Principles of State Policy
(Part IV) precedence over certain
Fundamental Rights (Part III), particularly
those under Articles 14 and 19.
• A new clause was added to Article 31C,
providing that no law implementing any of
the Directive Principles could be declared
invalid on the ground of violating Articles 14
or 19.
• Although the Supreme Court in Minerva Mills
(1980) struck down the extension of Article
31C to all DPSPs, it upheld the basic
precedence of Article 39(b) & (c) DPSPs over
FRs.
• This reflected the intention to promote social
and economic justice over individual rights in
certain contexts.
Hence, option (b) is correct

Q7. Which of the following is correct about the Chief
Election Commissioner? He holds the office till he
attains the age of
(a) 62 years
(b) 65 years
(c) 62 years or completes 3 years of service, whichever
is earlier
(d) 65 years of age or completes 5 years of service,
whichever is earlier
Answer: (b)
Explanation:
• The Chief Election Commissioner (CEC) and
other Election Commissioners are appointed
by the President under Article 324.
• They hold office for 6 years or until they
attain the age of 65 years, whichever is
earlier.
• They enjoy security of tenure — removal only
like a Supreme Court judge (by a special
majority resolution of Parliament on grounds
of proved misbehaviour or incapacity).
• Their conditions of service cannot be varied
to their disadvantage during tenure.
• They do not hold office “during the pleasure
of the President” despite being appointed by
him.
Hence, option (b) is correct
Q8. Which of the following States are not covered by
the Panchayati Raj?
(a) Tripura and Sikkim
(b) Manipur and Assam
(c) Meghalaya and Nagaland
(d) Assam and West Bengal
Answer: (c)
Explanation:
• Meghalaya and Nagaland are not covered by
the 73rd Constitutional Amendment Act,
1992, which provides for the constitutional
status of Panchayati Raj Institutions.
• This exclusion is due to their distinct tribal
traditions, customary laws, and existing local
governance systems protected under Sixth
Schedule and Article 371A.

•

Sixth Schedule provides for Autonomous
District Councils, particularly in Assam,
Meghalaya, Tripura and Mizoram.
• Nagaland’s local self-government works
through traditional tribal institutions.
• Other North-Eastern states like Tripura and
Assam are covered by Panchayati Raj
provisions.
Hence, option (c) is correct
Q9. Which of the following is a ‘cognizable offence’?
(a) First offence committed by any person
(b) Offence committed by a minor
(c) Offence other than murder
(d) Offence which can be dealt with by the court
Answer: (d)
Explanation:
• As per Section 2(c) of the Criminal Procedure
Code, 1973, cognizable offences are those
where a police officer can arrest without a
warrant and start investigation without the
magistrate’s permission.
• These are serious offences, such as murder,
rape, dowry death, kidnapping, theft, etc.
• In contrast, non-cognizable offences require a
warrant and prior permission from a
magistrate for investigation (e.g., defamation,
public nuisance).
• The phrase “dealt with by the court” here
refers to offences under regular judicial
procedure, not private complaints.
Hence, option (d) is correct

UPSC PYQs - 1987
Q1. A Parliamentary bill passed by the Lok Sabha is
sent to the President for assent. The President sends
it back to Lok Sabha for reconsideration. If the Lok
Sabha again sends it back to the President without
making any changes, the President
(a) Must give his assent to it
(b) Can again send the bill for reconsideration
(c) Can take help of the Supreme Court
(d) Can arrange referendum
Answer: (a)
Explanation:
• Article 111 governs the President’s powers
regarding ordinary bills:
o The President may give assent, or
withhold assent, or return the bill (if
not a Money or Constitutional
Amendment Bill) for reconsideration.
• If the bill is passed again by Parliament, with
or without changes, and sent to the President
the second time, the President is
constitutionally bound to give assent.
• The President cannot return it again nor refer
it to the Supreme Court nor call a
referendum.
• This ensures that Parliament’s will ultimately
prevails and prevents a deadlock between the
legislature and the executive.
Hence, option (a) is correct
Q2. The Institute of Ombudsman was first
established in
(a) Norway
(b) Sweden
(c) Finland
(d) Denmark
Answer: (b)
Explanation:
• The word “Ombudsman” is of Swedish origin,
meaning “grievance person”.
• The modern concept originated in Sweden in
1809, when the Swedish Parliament
established the office of Justitieombudsman

to safeguard citizens against administrative
injustices.
• Functions of the Swedish Ombudsman
include:
o Addressing abuse of administrative
discretion.
o Investigating maladministration,
corruption, nepotism, and official
discourtesy.
• The Ombudsman system has since been
adopted by many countries, including India in
the form of Lokpal and Lokayuktas (Lokpal
Act, 2013).
Hence, option (b) is correct
Q3. India is a federal state because of
(a) Dual Citizenship prevalent here
(b) Dual judiciary
(c) Share of power between the Centre and the States
(d) Written Constitution
Answer: (c)
Explanation:
• Federalism is characterised by division of
powers between two levels of government —
Central and State — each constitutionally
independent in its sphere.
• In India:
o Legislative powers are divided into
Union, State, and Concurrent Lists
(Seventh Schedule).
o Executive and financial powers are
similarly divided.
o There are independent constitutional
mechanisms (e.g., Finance
Commission, Inter-State Council) to
manage this division.
• Dual citizenship does not exist in India (unlike
the USA).
• Dual judiciary isn’t an accurate feature (India
has a single integrated judiciary).
• A written constitution (option d) is a
characteristic, but not the defining reason for
federalism.
Hence, option (c) is correct

Q4. Governor of which State has been entrusted with
the special powers for the administration of tribal
areas?
(a) Bihar
(b) Madhya Pradesh
(c) Assam
(d) Arunachal Pradesh
Answer: (c)
Explanation:
• Sixth Schedule of the Constitution contains
special provisions for the administration of
tribal areas in Assam, Meghalaya, Tripura
and Mizoram.
• In these states, certain tribal areas are
designated as Autonomous Districts with
District and Regional Councils, enjoying
legislative, administrative and judicial powers.
• The Governor of Assam plays a key role:
o He can organise and reorganise
autonomous districts, alter their
boundaries, or change names.
o He also has oversight powers over
laws passed by District Councils.
• These powers are unique to Assam’s
Governor and not found in Bihar, MP, or
Arunachal Pradesh.
Hence, option (c) is correct
Q5. The Speaker of the lower house of a State can
step down by submitting his resignation to the
(a) Chief Minister
(b) Governor
(c) Deputy Speaker of the House
(d) President
Answer: (c)
Explanation:
• The Speaker of a State Legislative Assembly
holds office until the House is dissolved or he
resigns or is removed by a resolution of the
Assembly (Article 178–181).
• He may resign by writing to the Deputy
Speaker (similar to the Lok Sabha Speaker
who resigns to the Deputy Speaker).
• Removal requires a majority of all the then
members of the House with 14 days’ notice.

•

The Speaker does not resign to the Governor
or CM.
• When a removal resolution is under
consideration, the Speaker cannot preside
over the House but may participate and vote.
Hence, option (c) is correct

Q6. India has Parliamentary democracy system
because
(a) Members of the Lok Sabha are elected by the
public
(b) Council of Ministers is responsible to the
Legislature
(c) Of distribution of power between the Centre and
the States
(d) Of a single constitutional framework
Answer: (b)
Explanation:
• India has adopted the Parliamentary system
of government, both at the Centre (Articles
74–75) and in the States (Articles 163–164).
• The key feature of the parliamentary system
is collective responsibility of the executive to
the legislature:
o The Council of Ministers is collectively
responsible to the Lok Sabha.
o If the Lok Sabha passes a noconfidence motion, the entire council
resigns.
• Other features:
o Real executive is the Council of
Ministers headed by the Prime
Minister, while the President is
nominal executive.

o

There is fusion of powers between
the executive and legislature.
• Option (a) is a feature of representative
democracy, not specifically parliamentary
system.
• Option (c) is related to federalism, not
parliamentary form.
• Option (d) is too broad and not the core
reason.
Hence, option (b) is correct
Q7. Judges of a High Court are appointed by the
President with the consent of the
(a) Governor
(b) Governor, the Chief Justice of India and the Chief
Justice of the High Court concerned
(c) Governor and the preceding Chief Justice of the
High Court
(d) None of these
Answer: (b)
Explanation:
• Article 217: The President appoints High
Court judges after consultation with:
o The Chief Justice of India,
o The Governor of the concerned State,
and
o In the case of appointment of a judge
(other than Chief Justice), the Chief
Justice of that High Court.
• Chief Justice of a High Court is appointed
after consultation with the Chief Justice of
India and the Governor.
• Second Judges Case (1993): Supreme Court
held that CJ of India’s opinion has primacy.
• Third Judges Case (1998): Appointment must
be through collegium (CJI + 2 senior-most SC
judges).
• This ensures judicial independence,
preventing executive dominance in
appointments.
Hence, option (b) is correct
Q8. Parliament can legislate matters in the State List
(a) By wish of the President
(b) If Rajya Sabha passes a resolution by 2/3rd

majority
(c) Under any circumstances
(d) By asking the legislature of the State concerned
Answer: (b)
Explanation:
• Article 249: Parliament can legislate on State
List matters if the Rajya Sabha, by a 2/3rd
majority of members present and voting,
declares it is necessary in the national
interest.
o The resolution remains valid for one
year, renewable annually.
• Other circumstances where Parliament can
legislate on State List:
o Article 250: During a National
Emergency.
o Article 252: When two or more States
pass resolutions requesting
Parliament to legislate.
o Article 253: To implement
international treaties.
o Article 356: During President’s Rule in
a State.
• It is not dependent on President’s wish, nor
can it be done under any circumstances.
Hence, option (b) is correct
Q9. The maximum gap between two sessions of the
Parliament can be of
(a) Six months
(b) Three months
(c) Nine months
(d) Twelve months
Answer: (a)
Explanation:
• Article 85: The President summons each
House of Parliament from time to time, but
six months must not intervene between its
last sitting in one session and the date
appointed for its first sitting in the next
session.
• This ensures regular parliamentary oversight
on the executive.
• Parliament usually meets in three sessions
each year:

o

Budget Session (Feb–May)
Monsoon Session (July–Aug)
o Winter Session (Nov–Dec)
• A gap of more than six months would violate
the Constitution.
Hence, option (a) is correct

Q10. First non-Congress government to come in
power in any Indian State is
(a) DMK in Tamil Nadu
(b) Communist Party of India in Kerala
(c) CPI (M) in West Bengal
(d) None of the above
Answer: (b)
Explanation:
• In 1957, the Communist Party of India (CPI)
won the Kerala Assembly elections, forming
the first non-Congress state government in
India.
• E.M.S. Namboodiripad became the first
Communist Chief Minister.
• This was historic because:
o It was the first democratically elected
Communist government in the world.
o It marked the end of Congress’s
monopoly in state politics.
• DMK came to power in Tamil Nadu only in
1967.
• CPI(M) in West Bengal came later, in 1977.
Hence, option (b) is correct
UPSC PYQs - 1988
Q1. A political party is recognised as a national party
(a) When it contests elections in all the States of the
country.
(b) When it secures at least five per cent of the total

votes cast in a national election.
(c) If it captures power at least in three States.
(d) If it is recognised as a political party in four or
more States.
Answer: (d)
Explanation:
• As per the Election Symbols (Reservation and
Allotment) Order, 1968, issued by the
Election Commission of India, a political
party can be recognised as a national party if
it satisfies any of the following conditions:
o It wins 2% of seats in the Lok Sabha
(i.e., at least 11 seats) from 3
different States, or
o It secures 6% of valid votes in four or
more States in a Lok Sabha or
Assembly election and wins 4 Lok
Sabha seats, or
o It is recognised as a State party in
four or more States.
• Recognition gives parties privileges like
reserved symbols, free airtime on media,
consultation in setting election dates, etc.
• Contesting in all states (a) is not mandatory.
Capturing power in three states (c) is not a
criterion.
Hence, option (d) is correct
Q2. The Constitution of India provides for the
nomination of two members to the Lok Sabha by the
President to represent
(a) Men of eminence in arts, letters, science, etc.
(b) Parsis
(c) The business community
(d) The Anglo-Indian community
Answer: (d)
Explanation:
• Article 331 of the Constitution empowered
the President to nominate up to two
members of the Anglo-Indian community to
the Lok Sabha if he/she is of the opinion that
the community is not adequately
represented.
• The aim was to safeguard the interests of the
Anglo-Indian community, a small minority.

•

Originally, the Lok Sabha could have 530
elected members from states, 20 from UTs,
and 2 nominated Anglo-Indian members.
• Note: The 104th Constitutional Amendment
Act, 2019 abolished this nomination from
January 25, 2020.
• Eminent persons (a) are nominated to Rajya
Sabha under Article 80.
Hence, option (d) is correct
Q3. The allocation of seats for each State in the
present Lok Sabha is based on the
(a) 1951 census
(b) 1961 census
(c) 1971 census
(d) 1981 census
Answer: (c)
Explanation:
• Article 82 provides for readjustment of Lok
Sabha seats after every Census through a
Delimitation Act.
• The 42nd Constitutional Amendment Act,
1976 froze the allocation of Lok Sabha seats
to the states based on the 1971 Census, to
encourage population control measures.
• This freeze was extended by the 84th
Amendment Act (2001) up to 2026.
• The 87th Amendment allowed delimitation of
constituencies using the 2001 Census, but
without altering the number of seats per
state.
• Thus, the 1971 Census remains the basis for
the allocation of seats in Lok Sabha.
Hence, option (c) is correct
Q4. Who among the following have a right to vote in
the Vice-Presidential election but not in the
Presidential election?
(a) Ministers who are not members of either House of
the Parliament
(b) Elected members of the Upper Houses of the
States
(c) Nominated members of the Parliament
(d) Members of the Parliament who abstain from all

meetings of the House for a period of sixty days
without permission of the House
Answer: (c)
Explanation:
• Vice-President is elected by an Electoral
College consisting of members of both
Houses of Parliament (Article 66).
o It includes elected as well as
nominated members of Parliament.
• President is elected by elected MPs + elected
MLAs of all states and UTs with legislatures
(Article 54).
o Nominated members do not vote in
the Presidential election.
• So, nominated members of Rajya Sabha and
Lok Sabha have the right to vote for VicePresident, but not for President.
Hence, option (c) is correct
Q5. It is for any economic consideration involving
loss of freedom of contract leading to forced
employment. Choose the correct answer from the
codes given below:
(a) 1, 2 and 3
(b) 1 and 2
(c) 1 and 3
(d) 2 and 3
Answer: (a)
Explanation:
• Article 23 of the Constitution prohibits traffic
in human beings, begar (forced labour) and
other similar forms of forced labour.
• The term ‘begar’ means compulsory work
without remuneration, historically prevalent
under feudal systems.
• The prohibition includes economic
compulsion — forcing someone to work for
less than minimum wage amounts to forced
labour.
• The Bonded Labour System (Abolition) Act
further reinforced this prohibition by
abolishing bonded labour agreements.
• Any contravention is punishable by law.
Hence, option (a) is correct

Q6. Assertion (A): The Indian Constitution closely
follows the British Parliamentary model.
Reason (R): In India the Upper House of the
Parliament has judicial powers.
(a) Both A and R are true and R is the correct
explanation of A
(b) Both A and R are true but R is NOT a correct
explanation of A
(c) A is true but R is false
(d) A is false but R is true
Answer: (c)
Explanation:
• Assertion (A) is true: India adopted the British
Parliamentary System, characterised by:
o Nominal and real executives,
o Collective responsibility of the
executive,
o Leadership of PM,
o Fusion of powers.
• Reason (R) is false: The Rajya Sabha (Upper
House) does not have judicial powers; only
the Judiciary has that role.
o Rajya Sabha has legislative,
deliberative, and some special
powers (e.g., Art. 249), but no judicial
functions.
• Therefore, while the assertion is correct, the
reason is wrong.
Hence, option (c) is correct

o

If the total strength of the Lok Sabha
is 545, then absolute majority = 273
(50% + 1).
• This majority is usually required for:
o Formation of government in Lok
Sabha.
o No-confidence motions.
o Removal of Speaker or Deputy
Speaker.
• It is different from:
o Effective majority → majority of
effective strength (total strength –
vacancies).
o Special majority → used for
constitutional amendments, varies by
article.
o Simple majority → more than 50% of
members present and voting.
Hence, option (a) is correct

• Absolute majority means more than 50% of

Q2. The first Leader of the opposition to get
recognition in the Indian Parliament was
(a) Y. B. Chavan
(b) Ram Subhag Singh
(c) A. K. Gopalan
(d) A. M. Stephen
Answer: (b)
Explanation:
• In India, the Leader of the Opposition is the
leader of the largest Opposition party having
at least 1/10th of the total seats in the House.
• The position was formally recognised in 1969.

•

Ram Subhag Singh, a senior Congress leader
from Bihar who later joined Congress (O), was
recognised as the first official Leader of the
Opposition in the Lok Sabha in 1969.
• Later, Y. B. Chavan (1977–1979) became one
of the most prominent Leaders of Opposition.
• The Leader of Opposition was given statutory
status in 1977, entitling them to salary,
allowances and facilities equivalent to a
Cabinet Minister.
Hence, option (b) is correct
Q3. Cognizable offence refers to an offence where
(a) Arrests can be made without warrant.
(b) Arrests can be made with warrant.
(c) It is under the jurisdiction of a court.
(d) Police can register a case without formal
complaint.
Answer: (d)
Explanation:
• Section 2(c) of CrPC (1973):
o Cognizable offences are those where
police can arrest without warrant
and start investigation without prior
approval of a magistrate.
• Examples: murder, rape, kidnapping, dowry
death, theft, etc.
• Police can register FIRs suo motu, i.e., even
without a formal complaint.
• Non-cognizable offences require a warrant
and magistrate’s permission for investigation.
• Option (a) is partially correct, but option (d)
captures the core legal definition more
precisely.
Hence, option (d) is correct
Q4. No money bill can be introduced in the
legislative assembly without the recommendations
of the
(a) Speaker
(b) Governor
(c) Chief Minister
(d) Finance Minister
Answer: (b)
Explanation:

•

Under Article 207, a Money Bill can be
introduced only in the Legislative Assembly,
not in the Legislative Council (if present), and
only on the recommendation of the
Governor.
• Every Money Bill is considered a Government
Bill and can only be introduced by a Minister.
• Once passed by the Assembly, the Bill is sent
to the Council (if applicable), which has
limited powers — it cannot reject or amend,
only recommend, and must return it within 14
days.
• Ultimately, the Legislative Assembly has
overriding powers regarding a Money Bill.
• The Governor can assent, withhold assent, or
reserve the bill for President, but cannot
return it for reconsideration.
Hence, option (b) is correct
Q5. Which among the following is not a part of
electoral reforms?
(a) Registration of political parties
(b) Disqualifying the offenders
(c) Installation of electronic voting machines
(d) Appointment of the Election Commissioner
Answer: (d)
Explanation:
• Electoral reforms in India aim to make
elections free, fair, and transparent.
• Some key reforms:
o Registration of political parties under
Section 29A of the Representation of
the People Act, 1951.
o Disqualification of candidates
involved in electoral malpractices or
criminal offences.
o Introduction of Electronic Voting
Machines (EVMs) in 1998 to curb
booth capturing and improve
efficiency.
• The appointment of Election Commissioners
is governed by Article 324 of the Constitution
and is not itself an electoral reform — it's a
constitutional mechanism.
Hence, option (d) is correct
