Q1. Parliament or a State Legislature can declare a
seat vacant if a member abstains himself without
permission from the sessions for at least
(a) 30 days
(b) 60 days
(c) 90 days
(d) 120 days
Answer: (b)
Explanation:
• Article 101(4) (for Parliament) and Article
190(4) (for State Legislatures):
o If a member is absent from all
meetings for 60 days or more,
without permission, his seat may be
declared vacant.
• The period of sixty days excludes any period
when the House is prorogued or adjourned
for more than four consecutive days.
• This provision ensures accountability and
discourages absenteeism among elected
representatives.
• Similar provisions are included in the Rules of
Procedure of both Houses.
Hence, option (b) is correct
Q2. Assertion (A): Panchayati Raj institutions were
set up to decentralise planning to village level.
Reason (R): Village panchayats are in a better
position to have a proper appreciation of their
developmental needs.
(a) Both A and R are true and R is the correct
explanation of A
(b) Both A and R are true but R is not a correct
explanation of A
(c) A is true but R is false
(d) A is false but R is true
Answer: (a)
Explanation:
• Assertion (A): True. The 73rd Constitutional
Amendment Act, 1992 gave constitutional
status to Panchayati Raj Institutions (PRIs) to
ensure decentralised governance and
participatory democracy.

•

Reason (R): True. Village Panchayats directly
engage with people and can better assess
local needs, resources, and developmental
priorities compared to distant centralised
bodies.
• The Act included:
o Three-tier system (village, block,
district),
o Reservation of seats for
SCs/STs/Women,
o 11th Schedule with 29 subjects for
Panchayats,
o Establishment of State Election
Commissions and State Finance
Commissions.
• Thus, both statements are true and R explains
A correctly.
Hence, option (a) is correct
Q3. Consider the following statements regarding a
No-confidence Motion in the Parliament:
1. There is no mention of a No-confidence
Motion in the Constitution of India.
2. A period of six months must elapse between
the introduction of one No-confidence Motion
and the other.
3. At least 100 members must support a Motion
of No-confidence before it is introduced in the
House.
4. A motion of No-confidence can be introduced
in the Lok Sabha only.
Of these statements:
(a) 2 and 4 are correct
(b) 1, 2, 3 and 4 are correct
(c) 1, 2 and 3 are correct
(d) 1 and 4 are correct
Answer: (d)
Explanation:
• Statement 1: Correct. The Constitution does
not mention No-confidence motions. It is a
parliamentary practice derived from Rules of
Procedure (Rule 198, Lok Sabha).
• Statement 2: Incorrect. There is no such
restriction of 6 months between two noconfidence motions.

•

Statement 3: Incorrect. Only 50 members
must rise in support for it to be admitted, not
100.
• Statement 4: Correct. A No-confidence
Motion can be introduced only in Lok Sabha,
since the Council of Ministers is collectively
responsible to the Lok Sabha (Article 75).
• If passed by a simple majority, the Council of
Ministers must resign.
Hence, option (d) is correct

Q4. The value of a vote of a member of the
Parliament for the election of the President of India
is determined by dividing the
(a) Nation’s population as per the latest census by the
number of Lok Sabha members
(b) Population as per the latest census by the total
strength of the two Houses of the Parliament
(c) The total value of votes of the members of all the
State Legislative Assemblies by the elected members
of the two Houses of the Parliament
(d) Particular State’s population as per the latest
census by the number of members of Parliament
elected from that State
Answer: (c)
Explanation:
• Article 55 of the Constitution lays down the
procedure for calculating the value of votes
for Presidential elections:
o The value of votes of MLAs =
(Population of the state / Number of
elected MLAs) ÷ 1000.

o The value of votes of MPs =
Total value of votes of all MLAs of all States÷Total nu
mber of elected MPs (LS + RS)\text{Total value of
votes of all MLAs of all States} \div \text{Total number
of elected MPs (LS +
RS)}Total value of votes of all MLAs of all States÷Total
number of elected MPs (LS + RS)
• This ensures uniformity in the scale of
representation of different states and parity
between states and the Union.
• Nominated MPs do not participate in the
Presidential election.
• The latest census used is the 1971 Census, as
the seat allocation and vote values have been
frozen until 2026.
Hence, option (c) is correct

Q5. Assertion (A): Equality before the law is not
applicable to the President of India
Reason (R): The President of India enjoys special
powers and privileges under the Constitution
(a) Both A and R are true and R is the correct
explanation of A
(b) Both A and R are true but R is not a correct
explanation of A
(c) A is true but R is false
(d) A is false but R is true
Answer: (a)
Explanation:
• Article 14 guarantees equality before law and
equal protection of laws. However, this is not
absolute — the Constitution itself creates
exceptions.

•

The President of India enjoys constitutional
immunities under Article 361:
o No criminal proceedings shall be
instituted during their term.
o No arrest or imprisonment shall be
issued against the President.
o No answerability to any court for acts
done in the exercise of official
powers.
o Civil proceedings can be initiated only
after due notice.
• These privileges are to protect the dignity and
functioning of the highest constitutional
office, not to grant personal impunity.
• Thus, both A and R are true and R correctly
explains A.
Hence, option (a) is correct
Q6. Holding of elections for the Panchayats is
decided by
(a) The District Magistrate
(b) Constitutional mandate
(c) The State Government
(d) The Election Commission
Answer: (d)
Explanation:
• The 73rd Constitutional Amendment Act,
1992 inserted Part IX (Articles 243–243O) into
the Constitution, giving constitutional status
to Panchayati Raj Institutions.
• Article 243K:
o The superintendence, direction, and
control of the preparation of electoral
rolls and the conduct of Panchayat
elections is vested in the State
Election Commission (SEC).
o The SEC is headed by a State Election
Commissioner, appointed by the
Governor, and enjoys security of
tenure similar to High Court judges.
• Neither the District Magistrate nor the State
Government conducts these elections.
• The Election Commission of India conducts
elections for Parliament and State
Legislatures, not Panchayats.

Hence, option (d) is correct
UPSC PYQs - 1992
Q1. Which one of the following statements regarding
the election of the President of India is correct?
(a) A candidate securing the majority of votes is not
automatically elected
(b) The Supreme Court has no jurisdiction in any
doubt or dispute arising in connection with the
election of the President
(c) The Presidential election cannot take place when
one or more State Assemblies stand dissolved
because all the State Assemblies form part of the
electoral college
(d) The total value of the votes allotted to both
Houses of Parliament is much more than the total
value of the votes of all the States taken together
Answer: (d)
Explanation:
• The election of the President is governed by
Article 54 and the Presidential and VicePresidential Elections Act, 1952.
• The election uses proportional
representation by means of single
transferable vote, and voting is by secret
ballot.
• The value of votes of MPs is calculated by
dividing the total value of votes of all elected
MLAs by the number of elected MPs.
• The combined value of votes of Parliament is
indeed more than that of the States taken
together. This ensures a federal balance tilted
slightly towards the Union.
• A candidate must secure a quota (total valid
votes ÷ 2 + 1) to be elected. Merely securing a
“majority” of first preference votes doesn’t
guarantee automatic election if the quota
isn’t met.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q2. The introduction of Pre-natal Diagnostic
Techniques Bill in Parliament was prompted by the
increasing incidence of
(a) Female foeticide

(b) Failure of contraceptive devices
(c) Spurious drugs being used for prenatal
immunisation
(d) Unqualified persons diagnosing pregnancy in
villages by primitive methods
Answer: (a)
Explanation:
• Amniocentesis and similar prenatal diagnostic
techniques were widely misused in the 1980s
to determine the sex of the foetus, followed
by selective abortion of female foetuses.
• This led to public outrage, activism, and
debate across India.
• Consequently, Parliament enacted the PreNatal Diagnostic Techniques (Regulation and
Prevention of Misuse) Act in 1994, effective
from 1996, later amended in 2003 to include
pre-conception techniques.
• The Act prohibits sex selection before or after
conception and regulates diagnostic
techniques to prevent misuse.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q3. Which one of the following is not in the State List
under the Constitution of India?
(a) Fisheries
(b) Agriculture
(c) Insurance
(d) Betting and Gambling
Answer: (c)
Explanation:
• The Seventh Schedule divides subjects into
Union List (List I), State List (List II), and
Concurrent List (List III).
• State List includes subjects like public order,
police, public health, agriculture, fisheries,
markets, betting and gambling.
• Insurance falls under the Union List, giving
Parliament the exclusive power to legislate on
it.
• This arrangement ensures economic and
financial subjects of national importance
remain under Union control.
Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q4. Which one of the following correctly reflects the
position of the Upper House of the State Legislature
as compared to the position of the Upper House of
the Parliament?
(a) While one-third of the members of the Rajya
Sabha retire every second year, one-fourth of the
members of the Legislative Council retire every
eighteen months
(b) There is no provision for a joint sitting of the
Legislative Assembly and the Legislative Council in the
States for resolving deadlocks while there is such a
provision in the case of the two Houses of Parliament
(c) While no Bill other than a Money Bill can originate
in the State Legislative Council, no Bill can originate in
the Rajya Sabha
(d) While Rajya Sabha has twelve nominated
members, the State Legislative Council has none
Answer: (b)
Explanation:
• Like the Rajya Sabha, the Legislative Council
is a permanent body, not subject to
dissolution. One-third of its members retire
every two years.
• Key difference:
o There is no provision for joint sitting
of the Legislative Assembly and
Council to resolve a deadlock.
o At the Centre, Article 108 provides for
a joint sitting of Lok Sabha and Rajya
Sabha to resolve differences on
ordinary bills.
• A bill rejected by the Assembly ends; the
Council cannot override it. This shows the
Council is weaker than the Rajya Sabha.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q5. The Constitution of India borrowed the scheme
of Indian Federation from the Constitution of
(a) USA
(b) Canada
(c) Ireland
(d) UK
Answer: (b)
Explanation:

•

The Indian Constitution borrowed features
from many constitutions:
o British → Parliamentary system, Rule
of law
o US → Fundamental rights, judicial
review
o Irish → DPSPs, Presidential election
method
o Canadian → Federal scheme with a
strong Centre, residuary powers with
Union, Governor appointed by Centre,
advisory jurisdiction of SC.
• Article 1 describes India as a “Union of
States”, indicating asymmetrical federalism
like Canada — some states have special
status.
• Thus, India’s federal structure resembles
Canada’s more than the USA’s.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q6. A High Court can withdraw a case from a
subordinate court and can deal with the case itself if
it is satisfied that the case involves a substantial
point of constitutional law. Select the correct answer
using the codes given below:
(a) 1 and 2
(b) 1 and 3
(c) 1 and 4
(d) 2, 3 and 4
Answer: (c)
Explanation:
• Under Article 228, a High Court may
withdraw a case from a subordinate court if:
o It involves a substantial question of
constitutional law.
o The High Court may either dispose of
the case itself or decide the
constitutional question and send the
case back.
• The High Court’s decision on constitutional
matters is binding on subordinate courts in
its jurisdiction.
• This provision ensures uniform interpretation
of the Constitution within the state.
Hence, option (c) is correct.

UPSC PYQs - 1993
Q1. The abolition of the I.A.S. and the I.P.S. has been
recommended by the
(a) Dhebar Commission
(b) Kalekar Commission
(c) Kher Commission
(d) Rajamannar Commission
Answer: (d)
Explanation:
• The Rajamannar Committee was set up by
the DMK Government of Tamil Nadu in 1969
under the chairmanship of Dr. P.V.
Rajamannar.
• Purpose: Examine Centre–State relations and
recommend steps to make the federation
more balanced.
• The Committee submitted its report in 1971,
making radical recommendations such as:
o Abolition of All India Services (IAS,
IPS, IFS), arguing they represented
excessive central control over states.
o Establishment of an Inter-State
Council with real powers.
o Curtailment of the Union’s power to
dismiss State governments.
• The IAS and IPS were retained, but this
recommendation highlighted state-level
discontent with central dominance.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q2. The only instance when the President of India
exercised his power of veto related to
(a) the Hindu Code Bill
(b) the PEPSU Appropriation Bill
(c) the Indian Post Office (Amendments) Bill
(d) the Dowry Prohibition Bill
Answer: (b)
Explanation:
• The President’s veto powers are of three
types:
o Absolute Veto – refusal to assent to
the bill.
o Suspensive Veto – returning a nonmoney bill for reconsideration.

o Pocket Veto – taking no action.
In 1954, President Dr. Rajendra Prasad used
his absolute veto to withhold assent to the
PEPSU (Patiala and East Punjab States Union)
Appropriation Bill.
• Reason: The bill was passed after President’s
Rule ended, so it was no longer valid.
• This is the only instance of the President using
the absolute veto.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
•

Q3. The Chief Minister of a State in India is NOT
eligible to vote in the Presidential election if
(a) he himself is a candidate
(b) he is yet to prove his majority on the floor of the
Lower House of the State legislature
(c) he is a member of the Upper House of the State
legislature
(d) he is a care taker Chief Minister
Answer: (c)
Explanation:
• Article 54: The Electoral College for
Presidential elections consists of:
o Elected members of both Houses of
Parliament, and
o Elected members of the Legislative
Assemblies of States.
• Members of Legislative Councils (Upper
House) do not vote in Presidential elections.
• Therefore, if the Chief Minister is a member
of the Legislative Council, he is not part of
the Electoral College, hence cannot vote.
• Whether he is caretaker or has to prove
majority has no effect on eligibility.
Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q4. Among the four pairs given below, which one
consists of a correct combination of dignitaries who
became Vice-Presidents after having held diplomatic
posts like Ambassadors and High Commissioners?
(a) Dr. S. Radhakrishnan and G.S. Pathak
(b) Dr. S. Radhakrishnan and V.V. Giri
(c) Dr. Zakir Hussain and K.R. Narayanan
(d) B.D. Jatti and K.R. Narayanan
Answer: (b)
Explanation:
• Dr. S. Radhakrishnan:
o Served as India’s Ambassador to the
USSR (1949–52).
o Later became the first Vice-President
(1952–62) and second President
(1962–67).
• V.V. Giri:
o Served as High Commissioner to
Ceylon (Sri Lanka) in 1947.
o Became Vice-President in 1967 and
later the fourth President of India
(1969–74).
• G.S. Pathak and B.D. Jatti were not diplomats
before becoming Vice-President.
• K.R. Narayanan was indeed a diplomat, but
he became Vice-President much later; the
correct pair as per the question is (b).
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q5. Which one of the following is in the Concurrent
List in the Constitution of India?
(a) Population control and family planning
(b) Public health and sanitation
(c) Capitation taxes
(d) Treasure trove
Answer: (a)
Explanation:
• Seventh Schedule – List III (Concurrent List)
includes matters on which both Parliament
and State Legislatures can legislate.
• Population control and family planning is
specifically listed in the Concurrent List.
• Public health and sanitation → State List.
• Capitation taxes and treasure trove → State
List.

•

Central law prevails in case of conflict (Article
254), but States may also legislate.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q6. Which one of the following is a feature common
to both the Indian Federation and the American
Federation?
(a) A single citizenship
(b) Three lists in the Constitution
(c) Dual judiciary
(d) A federal Supreme Court to interpret the
Constitution
Answer: (d)
Explanation:
• Indian and American federations differ
significantly:
o India: Single citizenship, single
integrated judiciary, three lists.
o USA: Dual citizenship, dual judiciary,
two lists (Federal & State).
• Common feature: Existence of a federal
Supreme Court to interpret the Constitution.
o In India – Article 124 establishes the
Supreme Court as guardian of the
Constitution.
o In the USA – SCOTUS plays a similar
role.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q7. Among the four political parties listed below,
which one was the last to be formed?
(a) The Conservative Party in Britain
(b) The Democratic Party in USA
(c) The Republican Party in USA
(d) The Indian National Congress
Answer: (d)
Explanation:
• Conservative Party (UK) – founded 1834.
• Democratic Party (USA) – founded around
1828 (oldest active party).
• Republican Party (USA) – founded 1854.
• Indian National Congress – founded in 1885,
during British rule.

•

It became the principal organization of the
freedom movement and later dominant
political party post-independence.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q8. Which one of the following comes under the
jurisdiction of both the High Courts and the Supreme
Court?
(a) Disputes between the Centre and the States
(b) Disputes between the States inter se
(c) Protection of the Fundamental Rights
(d) Protection against the violation of the Constitution
Answer: (c)
Explanation:
• Article 32: Supreme Court’s power to issue
writs for enforcement of Fundamental Rights.
• Article 226: High Courts have power to issue
writs for enforcement of Fundamental Rights
and other legal rights.
• Hence, jurisdiction over Fundamental Rights is
concurrent between High Courts and
Supreme Court.
• Disputes between Centre and States or States
inter se fall under original jurisdiction of the
Supreme Court only (Article 131).
Hence, option (c) is correct.
UPSC PYQs - 1995
Q1. Consider the table given below providing some
details of the results of the election to the Karnataka
State Legislative Assembly held in December, 1994.
Political
Party

Percentage of popular Number of
votes obtained
seats secured

Janata Dal 36

116

Congress

31

35

BJP

20.4

40

In terms of electoral analysis, the voter-seat distortion
is to be explained as the result of the adoption of the
(a) Hare system
(b) Cumulative vote system
(c) First-past-the-post system
(d) Plural vote system
Answer: (c)

Explanation:
• India follows the First-Past-The-Post (FPTP)
electoral system, also called the simple
majority system.
• Under this system, the candidate who
secures the highest number of votes in a
constituency wins, regardless of whether they
have an absolute majority (>50%).
• This often leads to voter-seat distortion, i.e.,
the proportion of seats won does not match
the proportion of votes polled.
• In the example, Janata Dal won 36% of votes
but secured 116 seats, whereas Congress
with 31% got only 35 seats, highlighting the
distortion.
• This is a known feature of FPTP — it favors
larger parties in concentrated areas and can
disadvantage dispersed vote bases.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q2. Which one of the following statements regarding
‘Exit Poll’ is correct?
(a) ‘Exit Poll’ is a term used to denote a post-election
survey of voters regarding the candidate in whose
favour they had exercised their franchise
(b) ‘Exit Poll’ and ‘Opinion Poll’ are one and the same
(c) ‘Exit Poll’ is a device through which results of
voting can be most exactly predicted
(d) ‘Exit Poll’ is an administrative device made recently
by the Chief Election Commissioner to prevent
impersonation
Answer: (a)
Explanation:
• Exit Poll refers to a post-election survey
conducted immediately after voters have cast
their votes, asking them whom they voted
for.
• It is different from Opinion Poll, which is
conducted before the election, to gauge likely
voting trends.
• Exit polls attempt to predict the likely
outcome of elections based on responses
from voters leaving polling booths.
• In India, the Election Commission regulates
exit polls, and publishing their results during

the election process is restricted to ensure
fair elections.
• They are not official results and can
sometimes be inaccurate.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q3. Which one of the following is correct in respect
of the commencement of the election process in
India?
(a) The recommendation for election is made by the
government and the notification for election is issued
by the Election Commission
(b) The recommendation for election is made by the
Election Commission and the notification for election
is issued by the Home Ministry at the Centre and
Home Departments in the States
(c) The recommendation for election is made by the
Election Commission and the notification for election
is issued by the President and Governors of the States
concerned
(d) Both the exercises of making a recommendation
for election and that of issuing a notification in
respect of it are done by Election Commission
Answer: (a)
Explanation:
• The election process begins with a
notification calling upon constituencies to
elect members.
• Government recommends the holding of
elections (usually after completion or
premature dissolution of the House).
• The Election Commission then issues
notification to schedule the election
programme.
• For Lok Sabha elections, the President issues
the formal notification, but based on the
advice of the Election Commission, and
similarly, the Governor issues it for State
Assembly elections.
• The Election Commission of India (ECI) plays a
central role in scheduling, supervising, and
conducting elections.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q4. Who among the following have the right to vote
in the elections to both Lok Sabha and Rajya Sabha?
(a) Elected members of the Lower House of the
Parliament
(b) Elected members of the Upper House of the
Parliament
(c) Elected members of the Upper House of the State
Legislature
(d) Elected members of the Lower House of the State
Legislature
Answer: (d)
Explanation:
• Elected members of the State Legislative
Assemblies (Vidhan Sabha) vote in:
o Lok Sabha elections → As electors
(citizens).
o Rajya Sabha elections → As part of
the electoral college, through single
transferable vote.
• Elected MPs (Lok Sabha) vote in Presidential
elections but not in Rajya Sabha elections.
• Upper House members (both Parliament &
State Legislative Council) do not vote in Lok
Sabha elections.
Hence, option (d) is correct.

Q5. Which one of the following States of India does
not have a Legislative Council so far even though the
Constitution (Seventh Amendment) Act, 1956
provides for it?
(a) Maharashtra
(b) Bihar
(c) Karnataka
(d) Madhya Pradesh
Answer: (d)
Explanation:

•

Legislative Council (Vidhan Parishad) exists in
only a few states: Andhra Pradesh, Bihar,
Karnataka, Maharashtra, Telangana, Uttar
Pradesh, and Jammu & Kashmir.
• Madhya Pradesh, despite the constitutional
provision, has never created a Legislative
Council.
• Article 169 enables creation/abolition of
Councils but does not make them mandatory.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q6. Prohibition of discrimination on grounds of
religion, etc. (Article 15 of the Constitution of India)
is a Fundamental Right classifiable under
(a) the Right to Freedom of Religion
(b) the Right against Exploitation
(c) the Cultural and Educational Rights
(d) the Right to Equality
Answer: (d)
Explanation:
• Article 15 is part of Part III (Fundamental
Rights) under the Right to Equality (Articles
14–18).
• It prohibits discrimination on grounds of
religion, race, caste, sex or place of birth,
ensuring equality before law and protection
against arbitrary state actions.
• It allows for special provisions for women,
children, and socially & educationally
backward classes.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q7. In which one of the following States of India it is
legal for a Hindu male and illegal for a Muslim male
to have more than one living wife?
(a) Nagaland
(b) Mizoram
(c) Goa
(d) Arunachal Pradesh
Answer: (c)
Explanation:
• Goa follows a common civil code based on
the Portuguese Civil Code of 1867.

•

Under this code, polygamy is legal for Hindus
under certain conditions, but Muslims cannot
practice polygamy.
• In the rest of India, Hindus are prohibited
from bigamy/polygamy under the Hindu
Marriage Act, 1955, but Muslim men are
permitted up to four wives under personal
law.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q8. Agricultural income-tax is assigned to the State
Governments by
(a) the Finance Commission
(b) the National Development Council
(c) the Inter-State Council
(d) the Constitution of India
Answer: (d)
Explanation:
• Agricultural income is a State subject under
List II (State List) of the Seventh Schedule.
• Parliament cannot levy income tax on
agricultural income; only state legislatures
can.
• This division is directly provided by the
Constitution, not by any commission or
council.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q9. Which one of the following is incorrect in respect
of Local Government in India?
(a) According to the Indian Constitution, local
government is not an independent tier in the federal
system
(b) 30% of the seats in local bodies are reserved for
women
(c) Local government finances are to be provided by a
Commission
(d) Elections to local bodies are to be determined by a
Commission
Answer: (a)
Explanation:
• Local government in India is not a
constitutionally independent federal tier; it
functions under state control.

•

73rd and 74th Amendments (1992)
constitutionalised Panchayati Raj and Urban
Local Bodies, providing for:
o State Finance Commissions for
financial devolution.
o State Election Commissions for
regular elections.
o Reservation for women and weaker
sections.
• Still, they remain subordinate to state
legislatures, not sovereign tiers.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
UPSC PYQs - 1996
Q1. The power of the Supreme Court of India to
decide disputes between the Centre and the States
falls under its
(a) advisory jurisdiction
(b) appellate jurisdiction
(c) original jurisdiction
(d) constitutional jurisdiction
Answer: (c)
Explanation:
• The Supreme Court has three types of jurisdiction —
original, appellate and advisory.
• Its exclusive original jurisdiction (Article 131)
extends to any dispute:
• Between the Government of India and one or
more States,
• Between the Government of India and one or
more States on one side and one or more
States on the other,
• Between two or more States, if the dispute
involves a question of law or fact on which the
existence or extent of a legal right depends.
• In addition, Article 32 gives the Supreme
Court original jurisdiction in the enforcement
of Fundamental Rights.
• The Court can also transfer cases between
High Courts or withdraw cases involving
substantial questions of law.
Hence, option (c) is correct.

Q2. When the Chief Justice of a High Court acts in an
administrative capacity, he is subject to
(a) the writ jurisdiction of any of the other judges of
the High Court
(b) special control exercised by the Chief Justice of
India
(c) discretionary powers of the Governor of the state
(d) special powers provided to the Chief Minister in
this regard
Answer: (a)
Explanation:
• The Chief Justice of a High Court exercises
both judicial and administrative powers.
• When acting in an administrative capacity, his
decisions are not immune from judicial
review.
• Such decisions can be challenged under the
writ jurisdiction (Article 226) of any other
judge of the same High Court.
• Neither the Governor nor the Chief Minister
has control in this regard, and the Chief
Justice of India does not exercise direct
supervisory power over High Court
administration.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q3. According to the Constitution of India, the term
‘district judge’ shall not include
(a) chief presidency magistrate
(b) sessions judge
(c) tribunal judge
(d) chief judge of a small cause court
Answer: (c)
Explanation:
• Articles 233 to 237 in Part VI of the Constitution deal
with the subordinate judiciary.
• The expression ‘district judge’ includes:
• Judge of a city civil court,
• Additional district judge,
• Joint district judge,
• Assistant district judge,
• Chief judge of a small cause court,
• Chief presidency magistrate, additional chief
presidency magistrate,

•

Sessions judge, additional sessions judge,
assistant sessions judge.
• Tribunal judges are not included in this
definition.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q4. What is the system of governance in the
Panchayati Raj setup?
(a) Single-tier structure of local self-government at the
village level
(b) Two-tier system of local self-government at the
village and block levels
(c) Three-tier structure of local self-government, at
the village, block and district levels
(d) Four-tier system of local self-government at the
village, block, district and state levels
Answer: (c)
Explanation:
• The 73rd Amendment Act (1992) established a
three-tier Panchayati Raj system throughout the
country:
1. Village level – Gram Panchayat
2. Intermediate (block) level – Panchayat Samiti
3. District level – Zila Parishad
• States with populations below 20 lakh may
not have the intermediate level.
• Members are directly elected at all levels,
ensuring uniformity across states.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q5. Which one of the following statements is
correct? The Prime Minister of India
(a) is free to choose his ministers only from among
those who are members of either House of the
Parliament
(b) can choose his cabinet colleagues after due
counselling by the President of India in this regard
(c) has full discretion in the choice of persons who are
to serve as ministers in his cabinet
(d) has only limited powers in the choice of his cabinet
colleagues because of the discretionary power vested
with the President of India
Answer: (c)
Explanation:

•

The Prime Minister is the real head of the
Union executive.
• He has full discretion in selecting ministers,
allocating portfolios, reshuffling, and advising
the President on appointments.
• The President is bound by the Prime
Minister’s advice and cannot exercise
discretion in choosing ministers.
• Even non-members of Parliament can be
appointed ministers but must get elected
within six months.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q6. B.R. Ambedkar was elected to the Constituent
Assembly from
(a) West Bengal
(b) the Bombay Presidency
(c) the then Madhya Bharat
(d) Punjab
Answer: (a)
Explanation:
• Dr. B.R. Ambedkar, though a resident of
Bombay, was not elected from Bombay
Presidency.
• His supporters arranged for his election from
a seat in West Bengal.
• He later became Chairman of the Drafting
Committee, playing a crucial role in shaping
the Constitution.
• He is known as the Father of the Indian
Constitution.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Q7. Assertion (A): The word ‘minority’ is not defined
in the Constitution of India.

Reason (R): The Minorities Commission is not a
constitutional body.
(a) Both A and R are true and R is the correct
explanation of A
(b) Both A and R are true but R is not a correct
explanation of A
(c) A is true but R is false
(d) A is false but R is true
Answer: (b)
Explanation:
• The Constitution does not define ‘minority’,
but Articles 29–30 mention minorities based
on language or religion.
• The Minorities Commission was set up by an
executive resolution in 1978, later made
statutory by the National Commission for
Minorities Act, 1992.
• It is not a constitutional body.
• However, the fact that the Commission is not
constitutional does not explain why ‘minority’
is undefined.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q8. Which one of the following countries has more
or less evolved a two-party system?
(a) Sri Lanka
(b) Bangladesh
(c) Pakistan
(d) Myanmar
Answer: (b)
Explanation:
• Bangladesh has evolved a two-party dominant
system:
• Awami League (AL) – secular, centre-left
• Bangladesh Nationalist Party (BNP) – centreright
• Though smaller parties exist, national
politics is dominated by AL and BNP, often
heading coalitions.
• Sri Lanka has a multiparty system; Pakistan
has unstable multiparty politics; Myanmar has
historically been under military or one-party
rule.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

UPSC PYQs - 1997
Q1. In which of the following countries will the no
confidence motion to bring down the government
passed by the legislature be valid only when the
legislature is able to find simultaneously a majority
to elect a successor government?
(a) France
(b) Germany
(c) Italy
(d) Portugal
Answer: (b)
Explanation:
• Germany introduced the concept of
Constructive Vote of No Confidence in its
Basic Law (1949) to avoid the political
instability experienced during the Weimar
Republic.
• Under this system, a motion of no
confidence must simultaneously propose a
successor Chancellor, who must secure an
absolute majority in the Bundestag.
• If the motion passes, the incumbent
Chancellor is removed and the proposed
successor automatically takes office.
• This prevents opposition parties from merely
destabilizing the government without having
an alternative ready.
• It discourages frequent government
collapses, ensuring continuity and stability in
the parliamentary system.
• The system has been adopted by other
nations like Spain, Hungary, Israel, Albania,
Belgium etc.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q2. Which one of the following was NOT proposed
by the 73rd Constitutional Amendment in the area of
Panchayati Raj?
(a) Thirty per cent seats in all elected rural local
bodies will be reserved for women candidates at all
levels
(b) The States will constitute their Finance
Commissions to allocate resources to Panchayati Raj
Institutions

(c) The Panchayati Raj elected functionaries will be
disqualified to hold their offices if they have more
than two children
(d) The elections will be held in six months time if
Panchayati Raj bodies are superceded or dissolved by
the State government
Answer: (a)
Explanation:
• The 73rd Constitutional Amendment Act,
1992 gave constitutional status to Panchayati
Raj Institutions (PRIs).
• It mandated reservation of not less than onethird (33⅓%) of the total seats and offices of
chairpersons for women, not “thirty per
cent”.
• It required the State Finance Commission to
be constituted every five years to recommend
distribution of financial resources between
the state and PRIs.
• It mandated fresh elections within six months
if a Panchayat is dissolved before completing
its five-year term.
• Some states introduced a two-child norm for
PRI representatives, but this is not a
constitutional provision.
• The wording “thirty per cent” is factually
incorrect — the actual figure is one-third,
making option (a) wrong.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q3. Proportional representation is NOT necessary in
a country where
(a) there are no reserved constituencies
(b) a two-party system has developed
(c) the first-past-post system prevails
(d) there is a fusion of Presidential and Parliamentary
forms of governments
Answer: (b)
Explanation:
• Proportional Representation (PR) is designed
to reflect the share of votes in the share of
seats, which is especially important in multiparty democracies.

•

In a two-party system, electoral outcomes
under FPTP usually mirror the popular vote
proportions, so PR adds little value.
• PR is typically used where there are many
parties, to prevent over-representation of
dominant parties and under-representation
of smaller ones.
• Countries like USA and UK with two-party
dominance (Democrats–Republicans, Labour–
Conservatives) function well with simple
majority systems.
• PR in such countries would complicate the
system without changing outcomes
significantly.
• The absence of PR in two-party systems does
not lead to disproportionate results, unlike in
fragmented systems.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
Q4. State Funding of elections takes place in
(a) USA and Canada
(b) Britain and Switzerland
(c) France and Italy
(d) Germany and Austria
Answer: (d)
Explanation:
• State funding means financial support
provided by the government to political
parties or candidates, either partially or fully,
to contest elections.
• Germany and Austria have well-established
systems of public funding for political parties
and elections.
• The aim is to reduce dependence on private
donors, limit the influence of vested interests,
and curb corruption.
• In Germany, parties receive funds based on
votes secured and donations received,
ensuring a mix of public and private support.
• The system helps promote transparency,
fairness, and level playing fields for smaller
parties.
• Countries like the USA, UK, and Canada have
either no public funding or very limited

mechanisms, especially for political parties
themselves.
• Germany’s model is often cited as a balanced
and stable approach to political finance
reform.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q5. The concept of Public Interest Litigation
originated in
(a) The United Kingdom
(b) Australia
(c) The United States
(d) Canada
Answer: (c)
Explanation:
• Public Interest Litigation (PIL) originated in
the United States during the 1960s–1970s as
a means to promote public causes through
judicial action.
• It allowed individuals or groups not directly
affected to approach courts for protecting
public rights, such as environmental
protection or civil liberties.
• PIL was used extensively by civil rights groups,
consumer bodies, and NGOs in the US to
address systemic issues.
• In India, PIL was introduced in the late 1970s,
pioneered by Justice V.R. Krishna Iyer and
Justice P.N. Bhagwati.
• It became a powerful tool in the 1980s to
make justice more accessible, particularly to
poor and marginalized communities.
• PIL led to judicial activism, enabling courts to
address issues like bonded labour, prison
reforms, environment, and rights of
vulnerable groups.
• Unlike in the US, Indian PIL has a broader
scope and relaxed locus standi, making it
unique in global jurisprudence.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q6. If the Prime Minister of India belonged to the
Upper House of Parliament
(a) he will not be able to vote in his favour in the
event of a no-confidence motion

(b) he will not be able to speak on the Budget in the
Lower House
(c) he can make statements only on the Upper House
(d) he has to become a member of the Lower House
within six months after being sworn-in as the Prime
Minister
Answer: (a)
Explanation:
• Under the Constitution, the Prime Minister
can be a member of either House of
Parliament — Lok Sabha or Rajya Sabha.
• No-confidence motions are exclusively moved
and decided in the Lok Sabha, as it represents
the directly elected House.
• A Prime Minister who is a member of the
Rajya Sabha can participate in debates in the
Lok Sabha but cannot vote on the motion.
• This reflects the principle that executive
authority is collectively responsible to the
Lok Sabh.
• Several PMs — Indira Gandhi (1966) for a few
months, H.D. Deve Gowda (1996), and
Manmohan Singh (2004–14) — were
members of the Rajya Sabha during their
tenure.
• This arrangement maintains flexibility while
upholding parliamentary accountability.
• He does not need to shift to Lok Sabha unless
politically desired.
Hence, option (a) is correct.
Q7. In the Presidential election in India, every
elected member of the Legislative Assembly of a
State shall have as many votes… “population” here
means the population as ascertained by the
(a) 1991 Census
(b) 1981 Census
(c) 1971 Census
(d) 1961 Census
Answer: (c)
Explanation:
• The value of votes for MLAs is based on the
1971 Census population figures.
• The 42nd Amendment (1976) froze seat
allocation and population figures until 2001,

later extended to 2026 by the 84th
Amendment.
• The freeze was intended to incentivize states
for population control.
• This ensures uniformity and stability in
presidential elections.
• The 1971 base continues to be used despite
later censuses.
Hence, option (c) is correct.
Q8. Assertion (A): The reservation of thirty-three per
cent of seats for women in Parliament and State
Legislatures does not require Constitutional
amendment.
Reason (R): Political parties contesting elections can
allocate thirty-three per cent of seats they contest to
women candidates without any Constitutional
amendment.
(a) Both A and R are true and R is the correct
explanation of A
(b) Both A and R are true, but R is not a correct
explanation of A
(c) A is true, but R is false
(d) A is false, but R is true
Answer: (d)
Explanation:
• Reservation of seats in legislatures requires a
constitutional amendment, like the 73rd and
74th Amendments did for Panchayats and
Municipalities.
• Political parties can voluntarily allot 33% of
tickets to women without constitutional
changes.
• There is currently no constitutional provision
reserving seats for women in Parliament or
State Assemblies.
• The 108th Amendment Bill (2008) proposed it
but remains unpassed.
• So A is false, R is true.
Hence, option (d) is correct.
Q9. In which one of the following countries are 75
per cent of seats in both Houses of Parliament filled
on the basis of first-past-the-post system and 25 per
cent on the basis of Proportional Representation

•

system of elections?
(a) Germany
(b) Italy
(c) France
(d) Russia
Answer: (a)
Explanation:
• Germany follows a mixed-member electoral
system.
• Around half to three-fourths of seats are
filled through FPTP (constituency) elections.
• The remaining seats are allocated through
Proportional Representation (party lists).
• This ensures local representation plus overall
proportionality in the Bundestag.
• Parties must cross a 5% threshold or win 3
constituencies to qualify for PR allocation.
Hence, option (a) is correct.

Constitution-making and its formal
enactment.
• January 26 was chosen for its symbolic
significance in the freedom struggle.
Hence, option (b) is correct.

Q10. In the following quotation “WE, THE PEOPLE OF
INDIA, having solemnly resolved to constitute India
into a Sovereign Socialist Secular Democratic
Republic and to secure to all its citizens: JUSTICE,
social, economic and political; LIBERTY of thought,
expression, belief, faith and worship; EQUALITY of
status and of opportunity; and to promote among
them all; FRATERNITY assuring the dignity of the
individual and the unity and integrity of the Nation;
In our Constituent Assembly this ‘X’ do hereby adopt,
enact and give to ourselves this Constitution.” … ‘X’
stands for
(a) twenty-sixth day of January, 1950
(b) twenty-sixth day of November, 1949
(c) twenty-sixth day of January, 1949
(d) None of the above
Answer: (b)
Explanation:
• The Constitution was adopted on 26
November 1949 by the Constituent Assembly.
• It came into effect on 26 January 1950,
commemorating Purna Swaraj Day (1930).
• The adoption date is explicitly mentioned in
the Preamble.

Codes:
ABCD
(a) 3 4 1 2
(b) 4 2 3 1
(c) 3 2 1 4
(d) 4 3 2 1
Answer: (b)
Explanation:
• The President takes an oath to uphold the
Constitution and the law (Art. 60).
• Judges of the Supreme Court swear to
faithfully discharge their duties (Art. 124).
• Members of Parliament swear to bear true
faith and allegiance to the Constitution (Art.
99).
• Union Ministers take two oaths — of office
and of secrecy (Art. 75).
• These oaths ensure accountability to the
Constitution and democratic principles.
Hence, option (b) is correct.
